Modern Indian History

All mnemonics in one place

753 memory hooks for wars, treaties, years, lists and sequences, taken from Chapters 1 to 18. Built only on facts in the notes. Reign-year codes are kept as in the chapters.

Chapter 0: Start Here: How to Use This Notebook (1)open chapter

2 · The box colours

Memory hook

Purple box: a memory hook (acronym, rhyme, story or peg) for a war, treaty, year, list or sequence. Each chapter also ends its notes with one "chapter in one breath" story. The Mnemonics page collects all of them.

Chapter 1: The Advent of the Mughals (51)open chapter

Power and its six forms

Memory hook

Six forms of power.

"Every Pandit Makes Sweet Tea Slowly."

Decode: Economic, Political, Military, Social, Tech (Intellectual/Technological), Spiritual, in the chapter's order.

Why it sticks: a pandit slowly stirring sweet tea is easy to picture, and the six words keep the order.

It is in the nature of power to centralise in a strong personality

Memory hook

Vertical vs horizontal (when power leaves).

Vertical = the Vault door opens downward; Horizontal = a Heir steps in beside.

Decode: Vertical = power flows down, so decentralisation (grandparents to the family). Horizontal = another personality replaces the weak one at the same level (Kodak → smartphones).

Why it sticks: V goes Down (V points down), H goes Across (H has a crossbar).

Country, nation, state

Memory hook

Country, Nation, State: C-T, N-P, S-O.

"Country = Territory, Nation = People, State = Organisation."

Decode: Country (Desh) is the land; Nation (Rashtra) is the people who are one, equal and supreme; State (Rajya) is the organisation that rules both.

Why it sticks: the State is the only one that does something (rules); the other two just are.

Types of State

Memory hook

Five types of State.

"My Police Keep Calm Nightly."

Decode: Military, Police, Kingdom/Empire (bloodline; an empire = a sum of kingdoms), Company (EIC, 1765–1858), Nation State (set up by the people).

Why it sticks: the list runs from force (Military, Police) to bloodline to business to the people, in the chapter's order.

Sovereignty

Memory hook

External functions: D, C, FA.

"Defence fights, Communications and Foreign Affairs talk and trade at the MEA."

Decode: D (Defence) → Defence Minister → war. C (Communications) + FA (Foreign Affairs) → MEA → diplomacy + trade. Symbolic sovereignty = real power 0; L, E, J are the three powers over these and over domestic affairs.

Why it sticks: one minister fights, the other talks, and you only need two ministries for three letters.

Modern INM: the phases

Memory hook

INM phases: Pen, People, Gun, then Gandhi.

"The Pen wrote (1857–1905), the People marched (1905–20), the Gun fired (1907–47), then Gandhi led (1920–47)."

Decode: Moderates = Pen; Extremists = People; Revolutionaries = Gun; Gandhian Era 1920–47; the fifth phase is the Many Voices (working class, women, Dalits).

Why it sticks: Revolutionaries start in 1907 (overlapping the Extremists) and run to 1947, the same end year as the Gandhian Era.

How the teacher says to remember battles and wars

Memory hook

Remember any war: G-D-P-R.

"Great Drummers Play Rhythms" (Geography, Duration, Participants, Reason).

Decode: for every war ask where (Geography/place), how long (Duration, e.g. Seven Years' War), who (Participants), why (Reason, e.g. Austrian War of Succession 1740–48). A war = the sum of its battles.

Why it sticks: use it as a four-box grid every time a new battle appears in later chapters.

Passages: how regions connect

Memory hook

Passages: natural = P-S, man-made = B-T-C.

"Nature gives a Pass and a Strait; people build Bridges, Tunnels and Canals."

Decode: Natural land = pass (Khyber, India's North-West entry); natural water = strait (Gibraltar joins Europe and Africa; the Dardanelles). Man-made land = bridges and tunnels; man-made water = canals (Suez, Panama; Panama 1914 = key to the rise of the USA).

Why it sticks: Khyber is the only pass you need to name, Gibraltar the first strait.

2 · The Mughal monarchs: a quick reference

Memory hook

The baton relay in the reign codes.

Each code's second number is the next ruler's first number, like a baton passed on the track: Babur 26-30 → Humayun 30-40 ... Akbar 56-05 → Jahangir 05-27 → Shahjahan 27-58 → Aurangzeb 58-07 → Bahadur Shah 07-12 → Jahandar Shah 12-13 → Farukhsiyar 13-19 → Muhammad Shah 19-48 → Ahmad Shah Bahadur 48-54 → Alamgir II 54-59.

Decode: only two dropped batons. Humayun 30-40 to Akbar 56-05 (the Suri gap), and Alamgir II 54-59 to Shah Alam II 60-06 (starts 60). Then Shah Alam II 60-06 → Akbar II 06-37 → Bahadur Shah Zafar 37-57 is smooth again.

Why it sticks: if your next code does not start where the last one ended, you have found a gap.

Memory hook

The first six: B-H-A-J-S-A.

"Bhaji, Sa-ab?" = Babur, Humayun, Akbar, Jahangir, Shahjahan, Aurangzeb.

Decode: Babur 26-30, Humayun 30-40, Akbar 56-05, Jahangir 05-27, Shahjahan 27-58, Aurangzeb 58-07 are #1 to #6. The Later Mughals (#7 to #15) begin with Bahadur Shah 07-12 in 1707, the year Aurangzeb 58-07 died.

Why it sticks: 6 great + 9 later = 15 monarchs.

Memory hook

Humayun 30-40 = "Hum-do" (two reigns).

"Hum do reigns: 30-40, then back for 1555–56."

Decode: Humayun 30-40 is the only Mughal with two reign periods. Overthrown by Sher Shah 40-45 in 1540, in exile, back on the throne in 1555, dead the following year.

Why it sticks: "Hum-ayun" literally contains "hum", and he is the one who came back ("ayun").

Memory hook

The Mughal see-saw: up, down, up, down, up.

1526 UP (set up), 1540 DOWN (demolished), 1555 UP (re-established), Jan 1556 DOWN (Humayun 30-40 dies), 1556 UP (re-established).

Decode: the State is 1526–1858 with an intermission 1540–55 (the Suri gap, "a gap in the Mughal movie"). The handout's monarch list ends 1857; the teacher writes 1858, when the Company State ended.

Why it sticks: five beats on a see-saw; the two DOWNs are Kanauj-era 1540 and the death in Jan 1556.

Who were the Mughals?

Memory hook

Mother Mongol, Father Timur.

"Ma = Mongol (Genghis Khan 1206–27), Pa = Timur (1370–1405). The child chose Papa's name."

Decode: maternal side = Genghis Khan, Mongol Empire (1206–1368); paternal side = Timur (Tamerlane), Timurid Empire (1370–1507). They took greater pride in Timur, who captured Delhi in 1398, so they called themselves Timurids. "Mughal" = Persian corruption of "Mongol".

Why it sticks: pride goes to the ancestor who actually took Delhi.

Why Babur 26-30 turned to India

Memory hook

Babur 26-30's road: 83, 04, 12, 19, 26.

Born 83; Kabul 04 (poor); Fergana lost 12; attacks start 19; Panipat 26.

Decode: 1483 born in Fergana; 1504 captured Kabul (economically poor); 1512 Central Asian ambitions collapse; 1519 begins attacking the Delhi Sultanate with Ottoman gunners; 1526 First Panipat.

Why it sticks: after Kabul 04, the gaps are +8, +7, +7 (04 → 12 → 19 → 26).

First Battle of Panipat (1526)

Memory hook

First Panipat 1526: small gun, big result.

"A dozen thousand men, but bronze guns go BOOM: Lodi falls, a Kingdom is born."

Decode: Babur 26-30 had about 12,000 men and small bronze cannons (Ottoman gunners) against Ibrahim Lodi, last of the Lodis (1451–1526). The Delhi Sultanate (1206–1526) ended. Tech power beat numbers.

Why it sticks: Babur 26-30 = Kingdom (K), Akbar 56-05 = Empire (E), Aurangzeb 58-07 = greatest Extent (E): "K-E-E".

Why three great battles happened at Panipat

Memory hook

Three Panipats: 26, 56, 61.

"Twenty-six, fifty-six, sixty-one: Babur 26-30's gun, Bairam's win, Kala Amb for the last one."

Decode: 1526 (First, Babur 26-30 beats Lodi), 1556 (Second, Bairam Khan beats Hemu), 1761 (Third; the Kala Amb memorial in Haryana is for this one, not 1526). The first two share "15"; the third jumps to 17.

Why it sticks: Kala Amb = the late one, 61.

Memory hook

Why Panipat? Three locks: K-T-Y.

"Khyber door, Thar-Himalaya tunnel, Yamuna wall."

Decode: close to the Khyber Pass (invasion route); a narrow corridor between the Thar Desert (south) and Himalayan foothills (north); the Yamuna to the east rules out flanking.

Why it sticks: the invader enters the door, walks the tunnel and hits the wall right at Panipat.

4 · Humayun 30-40 and Sher Shah 40-45 Suri 40-45

Memory hook

Humayun 30-40's battles go A-B-C: C, K, S.

Alphabetical = chronological: Chausa 1539 (lose), Kanauj 1540 (lose), Sirhind 1555 (win).

Decode: Chausa and Kanauj were his two defeats by Sher Shah 40-45; Sirhind was his win over the Suris with Safavid help. The Suri dynasty (1540–55) fills the gap between K and S.

Why it sticks: C, K, S never swap places, so the quiz order is just the alphabet. Two Ls then one W.

Sher Shah 40-45's three reforms

Memory hook

Sher Shah 40-45's three Rs.

"Records, Rupiya, Road."

Decode: Records = Patta + Qabuliyat (revenue; precursor to Akbar 56-05's Zabti). Rupiya = silver coin of fixed weight and purity. Road = Grand Trunk Road, Peshawar to Sonargaon (Bengal).

Why it sticks: "P to S" for the road, Peshawar to Sonargaon. He ruled the Suri dynasty only 1540–55, fifteen years.

Memory hook

Patta vs Qabuliyat: "Qabool hai!"

The State hands over the Patta; the ryot says "Qabool hai" and signs the Qabuliyat.

Decode: Patta = title deed from State to ryot (his field, his name, the LR to pay; a check on over-extraction). Qabuliyat = acceptance deed signed by the ryot and given back, promising to pay the LR on the Patta. Sher Shah 40-45 was first to make it two-sided.

Why it sticks: like a wedding, both sides must say yes.

Humayun 30-40 returns, with Safavid help

Memory hook

Persia's bill: Shirt, Key, Poem (S-K-P).

Humayun 30-40 pays for Safavid help with a Shirt, a Key and a Poem.

Decode: Shirt = adopt Shia forms; Key = cede Kandahar to Iran; Poem = promote Persian culture (so an Irani group of Mansabdars follows). The help: 14,000 Iranian troops, win at Sirhind 1555.

Why it sticks: three things to hand over, one picture each.

5 · How the Mughal Kingdom was re-established (Panipat 1556)

Memory hook

1556: one year, three events, one code.

"Fifty-six: Humayun 30-40 falls (Jan), Hemu crowns himself, Bairam wins, and Akbar 56-05's code starts with 56."

Decode: Jan 1556 Humayun 30-40 falls from his library stairs; Hemu (from Rewari, ex-Suri commander) declares himself Samrat; Second Panipat 1556, Bairam Khan (guardian of 13-year-old Akbar 56-05) defeats him.

Why it sticks: Akbar 56-05's code begins with the year of the battle, and "Bairam = Guardian" because the boy was only 13.

Rank: Zat + Sawar

Memory hook

Zat = Zaat (status), Sawar = Sawari (riders).

"Zat is who you are; Sawar is who rides for you."

Decode: Mansab = post/rank, Mansabdar = holder. Zat = personal rank (seniority, status). Sawar = number of horsemen to maintain. Net rank = Zat + Sawar (2,000 + 3,000 = 5,000).

Why it sticks: "Sawari" is the Hindi word for a ride, so Sawar = horse-riders.

Memory hook

Anshu = Army, Piyush = Paper.

Anshu (the emperor's favourite) is high on Sawar, the army; Piyush (the distrusted) is high on Zat, rank only.

Decode: the teacher's two outliers on the Zat-Sawar graph. Most nobles lie on the rising line (more Zat, more Sawar). Favourite = Anshu (high Sawar); distrusted = Piyush (high Zat).

Why it sticks: the trusted get the Army, the distrusted get Paper.

How Mansabdars were paid: Jagir, Khalisa, Inam

Memory hook

Four land types: J-K-I-M.

Jagir = Jagirdar's; Khalisa = King's; Inam = I'm-chosen gift; Mashrut = Mission bonus.

Decode: Jagir land: revenue to Mansabdars. Khalisa: royal land, revenue straight to the King, pays Naqdi Mansabdars. Inam: gifted land for a beneficiary (exceptional talent). Mashrut Jagir: a temporary top-up for a difficult assignment, revoked when it ends.

Why it sticks: J and K are the big two; I and M are the small extras.

Memory hook

Four fingers and a thumb: 4/5 Jagir, 1/5 Khalisa.

The Mansabdars hold four fingers of the revenue; the King holds only the thumb.

Decode: four-fifths of all LR came from Jagirs, only one-fifth from Khalisa land (teacher's 80 to 20). So the richest single person was still the King.

Why it sticks: the thumb is one finger, and the King is one person.

Three rules for Tankha Jagirs

Memory hook

Tankha Jagir rules: T-C-C.

"Tossed around, Capped, Confiscated."

Decode: Transferable (King can rotate it or turn the holder into a Naqdi Mansabdar); Capped (collect only the fixed LR, not a rupee more); Confiscated on death (Jagir and wealth both). Result: Mansabdars spent lavishly.

Why it sticks: a tenant who knows the house will be taken back spends everything on parties.

Watan Jagir

Memory hook

Tankha = Temporary, Watan = Waris (heir).

"Tankha is Taken back; Watan is Willed on."

Decode: Tankha Jagir = salary grant, rotated at will, confiscated on death; Jagirdar takes 100% of the LR. Watan = hereditary, permanent, not rotated; given to Rajput chiefs and submitted rulers; holder keeps 90% and pays 10% as Peshkash (tribute).

Why it sticks: Tankha's salary split (teacher's example) is 30 for self, 70 for the army: "thirty for me, seventy for my men".

What held the system together, and its fatal weakness

Memory hook

Patron gives, client pledges, ghosts appear.

King gives rank + Jagir; Mansabdar gives loyalty + army. If the King cannot deliver the desired Mansab and Jagir, loyalty melts, and half the army becomes a ghost.

Decode: patron-client bond; loyalty is personal, not institutional (to the King, not the State or law). Teacher's end state: on paper a 2 lakh army, in reality 1 lakh. After 1707, Mansabdars who did not get their desired posts turned disloyal.

Why it sticks: "2 on paper, 1 in reality" = half is a ghost army.

Who collected revenue, and how was it supervised

Memory hook

Revenue relay: write, file, assess, audit, collect.

The Patwari writes (village), the Qanungo files (pargana), the Amil assesses (pargana), the Amalguzar audits (sarkar), the Muqaddam collects (village).

Decode: Patwari = village ledger keeper, non-state, no vatan right. Qanungo = pargana, state official, hereditary keeper of old rates (has vatan right). Amil = pargana assessor; Amalguzar = supervises the Amil at sarkar level. Muqaddam = village headman who collects.

Why it sticks: P-Q alphabetical (record keepers), then A-A (assessors), and the Muqaddam closes at the bottom with the money.

Memory hook

Zamindar collects, Jagirdar consumes.

"Z Zips it up; J Just eats it."

Decode: Zamindar = right to collect LR (non-state actor, keeps about 10% Nankar / Malikana). Jagirdar = right to consume LR from a land (Mansabdar).

Why it sticks: the collector is the middleman; the consumer is the master.

The zamindar's position

Memory hook

The power ladder in six vehicles.

Sky to ground: Helicopter, Ferrari, Fortuner, Honda City, Luna, Legs.

Decode: King = helicopter; Jagirdar = Ferrari; Intermediary Zamindar = Fortuner; Primary Zamindar = Honda City; Khudkasht = cycle / Luna; Pahikasht = on foot.

Why it sticks: both Zamindars drive family cars you see on Indian roads; the top two are showpieces and the bottom two have no engine.

Memory hook

Primary 100-300, Intermediary 1000-3000: add a zero.

The Intermediary Zamindar is the Primary Zamindar with one more zero.

Decode: Primary Zamindar = smaller, 100 to 300 villages. Intermediary = big, 1000 to 3000 villages. Each keeps about 10% (Nankar / Malikana). Intermediaries pass Khalisa LR to the King and Jagir LR to Jagirdars.

Why it sticks: ten times the villages, ten times the status, but the same 10% commission rate.

Two kinds of ryot

Memory hook

Khudkasht = "khud ka khet"; Pahikasht = "paon-paon" wanderer.

The Khudkasht stays on his own field (occupancy, not ownership); the Pahikasht goes on foot from field to field.

Decode: Khudkasht = same land year after year, cannot be removed, holds a Patta, but cannot sell or mortgage (King owns all land). Pahikasht = different lands each season, no occupancy rights, landless and rightless (vagrant / Banjara).

Why it sticks: "khud" = self, and the teacher's own picture gives Khudkasht a cycle and Pahikasht his feet.

Revenue farming: Muqatai, Ijaradari, Farming System

Memory hook

Why Ijaradari arises: Need or Greed.

"Need runs dry, Greed runs wild."

Decode: Need = war, bad harvest, State's LR falling short. Greed = a Jagirdar who wants to maximise earnings, or a weak Mughal State. Both want more LR (↑LR).

Why it sticks: two words, one reason each: pressure from outside (Need), appetite from inside (Greed).

Memory hook

The painting auction: 1 crore in, 1 crore profit.

The right to collect revenue = the painting; the Ijaradar = Bhaskar, who buys at ₹1 crore to sell at ₹2 crore.

Decode: auction, money paid in advance, best price through competitive bidding. Ijaradar's motive is profit: the bid is the investment, and the LR he squeezes must cover bid plus profit. He farms revenue, not land.

Why it sticks: seller smiles (advance), buyer smiles (profit), the peasant is the canvas that gets squeezed.

Memory hook

Who auctions, how long: "J or K; 3 to 5, teacher 3-4."

The auctioneer is the Jagirdar (on Jagir land) or the King/State (on Khalisa land).

Decode: contract length is 3 to 5 years in the handout; the teacher writes 3-4. Learn "3 to 5 (teacher 3-4)": both start at 3. Bidders are merchants, moneylenders, bankers; the Zamindar temporarily loses his Watan right to collect.

Why it sticks: Jagir and Khalisa matches the letters J and K, just like the four land types.

Memory hook

Revolt needs a Leader and Followers: L + F.

Both unhappy = strong (a general with an army). Only the leader unhappy = none (a general with no army). Only followers unhappy = weak (an army with no general).

Decode: under Ijaradari the Zamindar (leader) lost his Watan right and earnings, and the ryots (followers) have no Patta: both are unhappy, so revolt is strongest. King/Jagirdar (advance, best price) and Ijaradar (profit) are the happy ones.

Why it sticks: the revolt is the product of two things; set either to zero and it fails.

Memory hook

Churaman 95-21 Jat: the code starts with 95, the revolt is in 1695.

"Ninety-five in his code, sixteen-ninety-five on the calendar."

Decode: a Zamindar-led peasant revolt (about 1695) over high LR that led to the Jat Kingdom.

Why it sticks: his code's first number is the revolt's year ending.

Memory hook

Zamindar plants a banyan; Ijaradar sells the timber.

The Zamindar's family lives with the peasants forever, so he caps himself at about 10%; the Ijaradar signs a short contract and strips everything.

Decode: Zamindar = hereditary, permanent, capped (Nankar / Malikana, about 10%). Ijaradar = auction winner, 3 to 5 years, no cap. Example: pays the State a fixed 1 lakh, extracts 3 lakh, keeps the 2 lakh profit.

Why it sticks: "1 in, 3 out, 2 kept" is the Ijaradar's arithmetic.

Memory hook

Zabti = Zameen survey; Ijaradari = auction, profit, no Patta.

"Zabti: Zameen is measured, Zamindar collects, Patta protects. Ijaradari: bid, bleed, no bill."

Decode: Zabti fixes LR by detailed survey, settles with the ryot (Patta), collection by the Zamindar for a State-mandated commission. Ijaradari fixes LR by auction, settles with the revenue farmer, collection by the Ijaradar for profit, and the peasant has no protection.

Why it sticks: survey vs auction is the single contrast that explains every other row.

The Ijaradari story across history: rise and decline

Memory hook

The door story: strong shuts it, weak opens it.

Akbar 56-05 slams it; Jahangir 05-27 leaves it ajar; Shahjahan 27-58 lets in only ruined land (banjar); Aurangzeb 58-07 opens the window (wars); Jahandar Shah 12-13 flings it open in Punjab and Bengal (fertile, richest); Farukhsiyar lets it into the King's Khalisa; Hastings 73-85 gives it a name (1772 Farming System).

Decode: before all that, the Delhi Sultanate began it as Muqatai. Rule: abolished when the State was strong, revived when it was weak. Successor states Awadh and Hyderabad continued it.

Why it sticks: the door opens a bit wider with each weaker ruler.

Memory hook

The floor stays at one-third; the ceiling climbs.

Akbar 56-05: 1/3. Shahjahan 27-58 and Aurangzeb 58-07: 1/3 to 1/2. Later Mughals: 1/3 to 2/3.

Decode: the lower figure never moves; only the top rises: third, half, two-thirds. Wars need money, so the demand rises. Always a share of gross produce (cost of cultivation was subtracted only by a later British reform).

Why it sticks: picture a lift: the floor button never changes, the top floor keeps going up.

Memory hook

Hastings 73-85: 72, 73, 74 on three steps.

1772 Farming System; 1773 Regulating Act makes him GG; 1774 he formally assumes office as GG.

Decode: Governor of Bengal Presidency from 1772; the Farming System (formalised revenue farming) = Ijaradari. His code 73-85 starts at the Regulating Act year.

Why it sticks: one step per year, 72-73-74.

8 · Akbar 56-05's religious vision: Sulh-i-Kul, Ibadat Khana, Mahzar, Din-i-Ilahi

Memory hook

Akbar 56-05's religion ladder: 75, 79, 82, then peace.

"I Must Do Sulh": Ibadat Khana 1575, Mahzar 1579, Din-i-Ilahi 1582, Sulh-i-Kul.

Decode: Ibadat Khana (Fatehpur Sikri, debate; goal: mid-path, less conflict) → +4 → Mahzar (Emperor is the final word on faith) → +3 → Din-i-Ilahi (syncretic religio-political order, not a movement) → Sulh-i-Kul (universal peace, equal respect for all faiths).

Why it sticks: the gaps are 4 then 3, and the last step has no year because it is the principle that stays.

9 · Who actually introduced Jizya in India?

Memory hook

Jizya: Qasim Started, Aibak Systematised.

"Qasim Started it (8th century, Sindh); Aibak made it a System (13th century, Delhi Sultanate)."

Decode: Muhammad bin Qasim first imposed Jizya in Sindh during the 8th-century Arab conquest. Qutb-ud-din Aibak 06-10 and successors institutionalised it from the early 13th century.

Why it sticks: "first imposed" and "institutionalised" are the two exam traps; Q is first in the line, A second.

10 · Two maps to fix in memory

Memory hook

Founder vs expanders, land vs coast.

Babur 26-30 = Built the base, Barren of coast. Akbar 56-05 Added coastal trade, Aurangzeb 58-07 Added more territory.

Decode: Babur 26-30 = founder with no coastal territory. Akbar 56-05 and Aurangzeb 58-07 = expanders; Akbar 56-05 brought rich coastal trade into the economy (which is why Europeans arriving by sea mattered).

Why it sticks: one B for the base, two A's for the additions.

11 · The teacher's revision boards on the land revenue (LR) system (Lecture 4)

Memory hook

Admin ladder: Suba, Sarkar, Pargana (S-S-P).

"Sab Sarkari Paper": Province = Suba, District = Sarkar, Block = Pargana, then Villages.

Decode: Centre → Suba (province) → Sarkar (district) → Pargana (block) → Villages. Inside a Jagir the Zamindar has his Milkiyat and the Khudkashts sit on the rest.

Why it sticks: the S-S-P order matches the Amalguzar (sarkar) sitting above the Amil (pargana).

Memory hook

Field survey = P-R-L.

"Production, Rain, Land records."

Decode: (1) past trends of Production and LR, area-wise; (2) average Rainfall, droughts, floods and their frequency; (3) Land records (which ryot cultivates which field, field size, LR paid before). Kept by the Patwari (village) and Qanungo (block); used by the Amil and Amalguzar to assess.

Why it sticks: the three P-R-L boxes are what an assessor needs before naming a number.

Memory hook

Any tax system: A-S-C-D.

"A Smart Collector Deposits."

Decode: Assessment (how much) → Settlement (Patta with the ryot, Qabuliyat with the State, both parties) → Collection (mostly Zamindars, audited by Amil and Amalguzar) → Deposit (King's Khalisa or the Jagirdar Mansabdar).

Why it sticks: Settlement (the two documents) sits between the numbers and the cash, so nobody pays what was never agreed.

Memory hooks: chapter in one breath

Memory hook

The Mughal story in one breath.

Babur 26-30 comes through the Khyber with small bronze guns: Panipat 1526, a kingdom is born.

Humayun 30-40 loses at Chausa 39 and Kanauj 40 to Sher Shah 40-45 (Records, Rupiya, Road), flees, returns with Persian troops at Sirhind 55, and falls from his library stairs in Jan 1556.

Hemu crowns himself, but Bairam Khan wins Second Panipat 1556 for 13-year-old Akbar 56-05.

Akbar 56-05 builds the Mansabdari machine (Zat + Sawar, Jagir, Khalisa, Inam, Mashrut), runs revenue by Zabti (Patwari to Muqaddam, Patta and Qabuliyat, Zamindar 10%), and climbs his religion ladder 75, 79, 82, then Sulh-i-Kul.

But loyalty is personal: when the King cannot deliver the desired Mansab and Jagir, loyalty melts, the army turns ghost (2 lakh on paper, 1 lakh real), and weak rulers open the Ijaradari door, ending with Hastings in 1772.

Decode: every year and name above is in the chapter's own order: 1526 → 1539 → 1540 → 1555 → 1556 → 1575 → 1579 → 1582 → 1772.

Why it sticks: it is one family story: a conqueror, a runaway son, a boy emperor with a guardian, a machine, and its slow rot.

Chapter 2: The Decline of the Mughal State (1707 onwards) (34)open chapter

The five-part equation

Memory hook

Five parts of State power: P-E-M-S-T.

"People Eat Many Sweet Treats."

Decode: Political, Economic, Military, Social, Technological. Mughal decline hit all five at once.

Why it sticks: if you can taste five sweets at once, you can feel five powers weaken at once.

Two analytical tools (apply to every decline question)

Memory hook

Vertical drops, horizontal swaps.

"Vertical = Vacate downward; Horizontal = Hand over sideways."

Decode: Vertical = decentralisation: after 1707 power moved from the weak emperors down to the Mansabdars. Horizontal = replacement at the same level: Delhi Sultanate replaced by the Mughals at Panipat 1526; British super-power replaced by US-USSR at the end of WW2 (1945).

Why it sticks: use both lenses on every decline question: one for who lost power downward, one for who took the seat.

The teacher's components board (what each form of power means)

Memory hook

Military power's four parts: S-T-C-C.

"Sena, Takneek, Clean hands, Commander."

Decode: Size of the army; Technology; no military Corruption; strong military Commander (leadership). Teacher's examples: 1945, USSR had the largest army; 1945, atomic bomb (USA) = technology.

Why it sticks: two numbers-and-guns items (Sena, Takneek), two people items (Clean, Commander); the Mughals later failed all four.

The teacher's list of factors

Memory hook

Six factors: "A Weak Mughal Is Just Lost".

A = Aurangzeb 58-07 (wars, religious policy); W = Weak emperors post-1707; M = Military weakened (Nadir Shah, Marathas); I = Institutional decline (disloyal Mansabdars, military corruption); J = Jagirdari crisis; L = Lack of reform.

Decode: the letters follow the teacher's numbered list 1 to 6.

Why it sticks: the sentence tells the whole result: the Mughal was lost.

2 · Why did Aurangzeb 58-07's wars sow the seeds of decline?

Memory hook

War is a sprint to spend, a marathon to earn.

Money leaves fast (men, horses, weapons, logistics, attrition); returns arrive slowly (territory, LR, ports, trade, a natural frontier).

Decode: war = rapid investment; victory = long, slow ROI. A war helps only if ROI is positive and peace follows. Aurangzeb 58-07's wars never stopped, so territory won was eventually lost: an economic disaster.

Why it sticks: sprint out, marathon back, and he never reached the finish line.

What he won, and what it cost

Memory hook

Bijapur then Golconda: B before G, 86 before 87.

Dynasty ends = annexation year: Adil Shahi 1489–1686 (Bijapur); Qutb Shahi 1518–1687 (Golconda).

Decode: Bijapur annexed 1686, Golconda 1687. Adil (A) is older (1489) and Qutb (Q) younger (1518). Both were successors of the Bahmani Sultanate (1347–1527). Bijapur was a three-way fight: Mughals, Bijapur, Marathas.

Why it sticks: alphabetical order matches chronological order, both times (A then Q; B then G).

Memory hook

Aurangzeb 58-07 in four lines: R-E-J-W.

"Rigid Emperor Jams Wallets."

Decode: Religious orthodoxy (Jizya 1679, temple policy); Executions (GTB 1675, Sambhaji 80-89 in 1689); Jagirdari crisis; continuous Wars (about 45 years). First two make social power sad; wars make the economy sad.

Why it sticks: four words, four lines of the teacher's board.

Memory hook

Akbar 56-05 = three smiles, Aurangzeb 58-07 = three frowns.

Akbar 56-05's three smiles: social (universal peace, religious harmony), economic (fair LR, no Ijaradari), military (Mansabdari machinery).

Decode: both ruled about 50 years. Akbar 56-05 = foundation of rise; Aurangzeb 58-07 = foundation of decline (social sad, economy sad, weak military, future emperors inherit a weak treasury).

Why it sticks: same length of reign, opposite faces.

Memory hook

Gol Gumbaz is a grave; Golconda is a fort.

"Gumbaz holds a body (Bijapur); Golconda is a fort (Hyderabad)."

Decode: Gol Gumbaz = mausoleum of Muhammad Adil Shah (1627–56), Adil Shahi dynasty, Bijapur. Golconda Fort, Hyderabad = seat of the Qutb Shahi dynasty.

Why it sticks: both start with "Gol", so pin each to a different dynasty: Gumbaz → Adil, Golconda → Qutb.

3.1 Jizya (1679) and its long history

Memory hook

First vs formal: Qasim is Quick, Aibak is Administrative.

"Qasim Quick (712 CE, Sindh); Aibak = Administration (institutionalised)."

Decode: Muhammad bin Qasim introduced it first, after conquering Sindh in 712 CE; payers became Zimmis (protected people), allowed their religion and exempt from military service. Aibak 06-10 first institutionalised it.

Why it sticks: two different verbs, two different men: "introduced" vs "institutionalised".

Memory hook

Jizya's light switch: ON, ON, OFF, ON, OFF, ON, fade.

Qasim 712 ON; Aibak 06-10 ON (institutional); Akbar 56-05 OFF 1564; Aurangzeb 58-07 ON 1679; Zulfiqar Khan OFF 1712; Farukhsiyar 13-19 ON 1717; fades 1719.

Decode: Babur 26-30 and Humayun 30-40 continued it. Off 64, on 79, off 12, on 17. Handout says Farukhsiyar re-introduced it in 1717; the teacher's slide says he continued the abolition but later re-imposed it. Both agree: abolished 1712, re-imposed by Farukhsiyar, gone by 1719.

Why it sticks: three flips back and forth, then the bulb dies with the empire.

3.2 Temple policy: Kashi Vishwanath (1669)

Memory hook

Kashi Vishwanath: three break, one builds (11, 14, 16, 17).

"Aibak Sikandar Aurangzeb break; Ahilyabai builds": 1194, 1490, 1669, 1780.

Decode: Aibak 06-10 razed it in 1194 (then Ghori's slave-general); Sikandar Lodi in 1490; Aurangzeb 58-07 demolished it in 1669 (Gyanvapi Mosque on the site; court record Maasir-i-Alamgiri); Ahilyabai Holkar of Indore rebuilt it in 1780 near the original site.

Why it sticks: the first two digits climb 11 → 14 → 16 → 17, and the builder comes last.

3.3 Executions that backfired

Memory hook

Martyr codes: Sambhaji 80-89 ends where he was executed.

GTB = 9th Sikh Guru, 1675. Sambhaji 80-89, elder son of Shivaji 74-80, executed 1689: his code ends in 89.

Decode: both were offered convert-or-die, both refused; Sambhaji 80-89 after weeks of torture. Executions created martyrs and hardened Sikh and Maratha anti-Mughal feeling.

Why it sticks: the end of his code is the year of his death.

Memory hook

Aurangzeb 58-07's four black dates: 69, 75, 79, 89.

"Kashi, Guru, Jizya, Sambhaji" = 1669, 1675, 1679, 1689.

Decode: Kashi Vishwanath demolished 1669; Guru Tegh Bahadur executed 1675; Jizya re-introduced 1679; Sambhaji 80-89 executed 1689. All four hit social power.

Why it sticks: steps of +6, +4, +10, and the rhythm "sixty-nine, seventy-five, seventy-nine, eighty-nine".

4 · Why did the Mansabdari system turn against the empire?

Memory hook

Strong king = Freeze, weak king = Free-for-all.

Strong emperor: Faith (good strong) or Fear (bad strong) makes the unhappy Mansabdar freeze. Weak emperor: the Mansabdar is free to act.

Decode: an unhappy Mansabdar (no desired Mansab or Jagir) acts only under a weak emperor: lobbying, backbiting, takhtapalat (coup) to bring his favourite from the royal family. Result: busy in court politics, not on work.

Why it sticks: the same feeling of disloyalty exists in both cases; only the king's strength decides whether it acts.

5.1 No technological or organisational reform

Memory hook

European tricks: File, Grape, Bayonet.

A file of men fire in turns; the shot sprays like grapes; the Brown Bess grows a knife, the bayonet.

Decode: file firing = rotating lines for continuous fire; grapeshot = many bullets in one shot, lethal at close range; bayonet on the Brown Bess musket turns a rifle into a sword. Haider Ali 61-82 (Mysore) reorganised on a European model (the slide says the French model, the handout says European) and stayed formidable.

Why it sticks: F-G-B runs from long range to close range to hand-to-hand.

5.3 Military corruption

Memory hook

Three military problems: N-W-C.

"No reform, Weak commanders, Corruption."

Decode: no technological or organisational reform; weak war commanders (court politics, not merit); military corruption (Mansabdars kept far fewer cavalry than their Sawar rank, pocketing the difference).

Why it sticks: the paper army and the real army do not match: Sawar on paper, fewer horses in the field.

6 · Factionalism: how it operated in the Mughal court

Memory hook

Five factions: "It's The Hot Hyderabadi Dum".

Irani, Turani, Hindustani, Hindu, Deccani.

Decode: Irani = Shia Persian, Zulfiqar Khan. Turani = Sunni Turkish-Uzbek (like the Mughals), Nizam-ul-Mulk 24-48. Hindustani = converted Indian Muslims, Sayyid Brothers (brought Farukhsiyar 13-19 to power). Hindu = Rajputs and Jats, Sawai Jai Singh 99-43. Deccani = Bijapur and Golconda nobles (no example given).

Why it sticks: "Z-N-S-J" gives the four examples in order: Zulfiqar, Nizam, Sayyids, Jai Singh 99-43.

7 · The Jagirdari crisis: why did it hit so hard?

Memory hook

Jagirdari crisis = water crisis.

More mouths, less water: more Mansabdars, less land.

Decode: demand-supply mismatch. Land shrank through territory loss (post-1705, Marathas winning); over-recruitment came from Aurangzeb 58-07's Deccani nobles and from post-1707 factionalism. The teacher: the impact of the Jagirdari crisis was the most important in the decline of the Mughals.

Why it sticks: you know the water crisis already; just swap people for Mansabdars and water for Jagirs.

Memory hook

Four causes: K-O-I-L (the Koel).

"Khalisa grab, Over-recruitment, Inequality, Luxury inflation."

Decode: after Bijapur (1686) and Golconda (1687) Aurangzeb 58-07 declared most of their lands Khalisa; later Mughals over-recruited to buy loyalty; 5% of Mansabdars controlled 61% of Jagir revenue; luxury inflation (partly from EIC exports).

Why it sticks: the Koel's nest is crowded and unequal, and "5 and 61" = 5% of Mansabdars hold 61% of the Jagir revenue.

The consequences

Memory hook

Kal ho na ho Jagir: the tenant who strips the house.

A tenant told he may leave tomorrow takes everything today: transfers, insecurity, ruthless extraction, Ijaradari, then revolt.

Decode: frequent transfers → tenure insecurity (kal ho na ho Jagir) → maximum in minimum time → Ijaradari spreads (Zamindars, the rural stabilisers, are bypassed) → Zamindar-led revolts. Churaman 95-21 Jat: the Jat Kingdom grew from his 1695 revolt, and the code starts with 95.

Why it sticks: five steps, one tenant, and a famous film-style phrase for the insecurity.

8 · Proof of decline: factionalism in practice

Memory hook

Succession turmoil = a three-round boxing match: R-R, R-N, N-N.

Round 1 Royal vs Royal: Bahadur Shah 07-12 fights his brothers; Jahandar Shah 12-13 wins with Zulfiqar Khan (Irani, made Wazir). Round 2 Royal vs Noble: Farukhsiyar 13-19 with the Sayyids. Round 3 Noble vs Noble: Nizam-ul-Mulk 24-48. Between 2 and 3: Muhammad Shah 19-48 the puppet.

Decode: the teacher corrected the turmoil from "1719-24" to 1707-19 (Aurangzeb 58-07's death to Muhammad Shah 19-48's installation). Farukhsiyar removed and executed Jahandar Shah 12-13; the Sayyids, allied with the Marathas, killed Farukhsiyar.

Why it sticks: the fighters get bigger each round, and the winner of each round is eventually killed by the next.

Memory hook

Sayyid Brothers: AW! HM!

Abdullah = Wazir; Hussain Ali = Mir Bakshi.

Decode: the brothers who made Farukhsiyar 13-19 emperor; he refused to be their puppet, so they allied with the Marathas under Peshwa Balaji Vishwanath 13-20 (15,000 cavalry took over Delhi in 1719), killed him and installed Muhammad Shah 19-48. Mir Bakshi = chief military paymaster/commander.

Why it sticks: "15,000 in 1719" repeats the 15 and the 19.

Memory hook

Nizam 24-48: his code starts the year he reaches Hyderabad.

"Wazir 22-24, then Hyderabad in 24, and he leaves Delhi for good in 1740."

Decode: Nizam-ul-Mulk 24-48 (Turani) allied with the Irani group and defeated the Sayyids; Wazir 1722-24; took the Mansab of Governor of Hyderabad Suba in 1724; left Delhi permanently in 1740. His mood: "Delhi to gayo, at least save my Hyderabad."

Why it sticks: Wazir ends exactly where the Hyderabad code begins (24).

9 · How the Marathas exposed the military collapse

Memory hook

Maratha steps: 05, 37, 38, 52, 54.

1705 winning; 1737 Bajirao 20-40 plunders Delhi; 1738 Bhopal; 1752 protection; 1754 puppet Alamgir II 54-59.

Decode: after 1705 the Marathas defeat Mughal forces; 1737 Bajirao 20-40 briefly holds Md Shah prisoner (military prestige down); 1738 they beat Nizam, and the Treaty of Bhopal gives them the lands between Chambal and Narmada plus Malwa. (The handout diagram prints 1739; the text and timeline use 1738, so follow 1738.)

Why it sticks: draw a sandwich: Chambal on top, Malwa the filling, Narmada below (the teacher's sketch).

What happened, step by step

Memory hook

Karnal 1739: 50 lakh to stay out, a snub to bring him in.

Nizam, now Mir Bakshi, buys peace for 50 lakh; Sadat Khan 22-39, passed over for that post, goads Nadir into Delhi; 60 days of Nadir's rule follow.

Decode: Karnal defeat, Sadat Khan 22-39 taken prisoner, Nizam negotiates (Nadir does not enter Delhi). Factionalism then breaks the deal: Sadat wanted the Mir Bakshi post that went to Nizam. Nadir's war aim was plunder, not annexation. Sadat's code ends in 39, the year of Karnal.

Why it sticks: one snub undoes a 50 lakh treaty.

The five pillars

Memory hook

Nadir's five blows: P-S-M-E-T.

"Please Save My Empire, Thanks."

Decode: Political = Md Shah humiliated; Social = about 2 lakh Delhi civilians massacred; Military = invited Abdali's first invasion of 1748; Economic = about 100 crore looted, Peacock Throne with the Kohinoor; Technological = no funds to close the technology gap.

Why it sticks: Nadir = the lowest point ("nadir") of Mughal power.

Memory hook

Nadir's numbers: 60, 2, 100, 50.

60 days of rule, 2 lakh killed, 100 crore taken, 50 lakh was the price of staying out.

Decode: 1739: Nadir ruled Delhi for 60 days; about two lakh civilians massacred; about 100 crore rupees looted (x250-300 for present value); Nizam negotiated 50 lakh at Karnal, which Nadir accepted to go back.

Why it sticks: the offer (50 lakh) is tiny beside the haul (100 crore).

Memory hook

Let the elephants fight: Marathas wait, then protect.

After Bhopal (1738) the Marathas went home to the South while Nadir hit Delhi in 1739; patience gave them the protectorate in 1752.

Decode: they had already extracted the maximum through the Treaty of Bhopal; a rival State was allowed to get weak; "why interfere unless there is a deal, like in 1752?"

Why it sticks: two elephants fight, the watcher collects the grass.

11 · India by the mid-18th century

Memory hook

Three kinds of regional power: BAH, JMRF, MRT.

Successors BAH: Bengal, Awadh, Hyderabad ("Bah!"). Rebels JMRF: Jats, Marathas, Rohilkhand, Farukhabad ("Just Make Rotis Fresh"). Already autonomous MRT: Mysore, Rajputs, Travancore.

Decode: the teacher's classification of the mid-18th-century map; Rohilkhand and Farukhabad are the Afghans. No. 1 power = Marathas.

Why it sticks: three groups of 3, 4, 3: successors, rebels, autonomous.

The Prelims 2003 pin: Shah Alam II 60-06

Memory hook

Shah Alam II 60-06: the danger was in the house, not at the gate.

"The enemy at home (Wazir Imad-ul-Mulk), not at the border (North-West frontier)."

Decode: Assertion A (stayed away from Delhi) is true; Reason R (foreign invasion danger) is false because the cause was his own Wazir, Imad-ul-Mulk. Handout and slides 36 and 37 say (c); the teacher's ticks point to (b) and coaching keys are split; UPSC gave no key. Hold the Wazir fact.

Why it sticks: "Imad" starts with "I" for Inside.

Teacher's lesson on Assertion-Reason questions

Memory hook

Assertion-Reason method: A-R-T.

"Is A true? Is R true? Then Think."

Decode: (i) read A alone and mark T/F; (ii) read R alone and mark T/F; (iii) only if both are true, THINK: is R the reason for A? On this PYQ the teacher boxed "always" in R and first ticked (b).

Why it sticks: ART is a three-beat routine: A, R, then the Thinking step that most people skip.

12 · Exam strategy: how to turn a PYQ into a FYQ

Memory hook

Mains 2017: the protagonist gets the 70.

PYQ (fragmented polity): 30 Mughal decline, 70 regional powers. FYQ (Mughal Empire's collapse): 70 Mughal decline, 30 regional powers.

Decode: ask "who is the question about?" The protagonist (fragmented polity in the PYQ; the Mughal Empire itself in the FYQ) takes 70%; the other side is only 30% support.

Why it sticks: 3-7 flips to 7-3 when the hero changes.

Memory hooks: chapter in one breath

Memory hook

The Mughal fall in one breath.

Aurangzeb 58-07 wins Bijapur 86 and Golconda 87, but forty years of war drain the treasury and his black dates (69, 75, 79, 89) cost him the people.

He dies in 1707 at the peak; the Mansabdars, now unhappy and unpaid in a Jagirdari crisis (5% hold 61%), turn to court politics.

Boxing rounds follow: Bahadur Shah 07-12, Jahandar Shah 12-13, Farukhsiyar 13-19 and the Sayyids, then the puppet Muhammad Shah 19-48 in 1719, while Nizam 24-48 slips off to Hyderabad.

The Marathas expose the rot: Bajirao 20-40 plunders Delhi 1737, the Treaty of Bhopal follows in 1738.

Nadir Shah arrives: Karnal, 60 days, 2 lakh dead, 100 crore gone, 1739; Abdali comes in 1748.

The Mughal ends up under Maratha protection in 1752 and gets a puppet, Alamgir II 54-59, in 1754.

Decode: 1707 → 1719 → 1737 → 1738 → 1739 → 1748 → 1752 → 1754 is the chapter's own chain.

Why it sticks: a rich man's house: he wins big, spends it all, his sons fight, burglars walk in, and the watchman (Marathas) takes charge.

Chapter 3: Regional States: the Successor States of Bengal, Awadh and Hyderabad (33)open chapter

0 · Control versus rule (the teacher's opening)

Memory hook

Plassey pockets, Buxar bosses.

Plassey 1757 = the EIC Pockets Bengal (control); Buxar 1764 = the EIC Bosses it (rule). Seven years from pocket to boss.

Decode: Plassey 1757 = control / conquest began; Buxar 1764 = rule began. Control without rule = absentee colonialism (Angola, Mozambique); rule = L, E, J plus governance.

Why it sticks: P comes before B, just as 57 comes before 64.

1 · Framework: the three classes of regional State

Memory hook

Heir, Rebel, Homeowner.

The Heir took the house quietly, the Rebel fought for a room, the Homeowner never rented from the emperor at all.

Decode: Heir = Successor States (Bengal, Awadh, Hyderabad: governors "stabbed him in the back" but never declared independence); Rebel = Rebel States (Marathas, Sikhs, Jats, Afghans of Farukhabad and Rohilkhand: they FOUGHT the Mughal State); Homeowner = Already Autonomous (Rajputs, Mysore, Travancore: the Mughal never ruled there).

Why it sticks: only the Rebel has a fight in the story.

The list of later emperors (teacher's proof of decline)

Memory hook

The queue of nine emperors.

Bear, Jaguar, Fox, Monkey, Ant, Alligator, Snake, Ape, Zebra: a zoo queue at the Red Fort gate.

Decode (first letters): Bahadur Shah 1707–12, Jahandar Shah 12-13, Farukhsiyar (FS 13-19), Muhammad Shah 19-48, Ahmad Shah Bahadur 1748–54, Alamgir II 54-59, Shah Alam II 60-06, Akbar II 06-37, Bahadur Shah Zafar 37-57.

Why it sticks: each reign ends where the next begins (12, 13, 19, 48, 54, 59-60, 06, 37), so you only learn the slips. The Fox (13-19) and the Monkey (19-48) are the reigns in which the Successor States arose.

2 · Why autonomous but never formally independent?

Memory hook

BAH! 17-22-24.

The emperor sighs "BAH!": Bengal, Awadh, Hyderabad, in that order, in 17, then 22, then 24 (gaps of 5 and 2).

Decode: Bengal 1717 (Murshid Quli Khan, MQK, 1717–27); Awadh 1722 (Sadat Khan 22-39); Hyderabad 1724 (Nizam-ul-Mulk 24-48). B-A-H is also alphabetical order.

Why it sticks: each founder's code starts with the same two digits as his founding year (17-27, 22-39, 24-48).

Memory hook

Sam Trades safely.

S-A-M-T: why B, A, H never declared independence.

Decode: Symbolic legitimacy (social power); Ambitions at Delhi (political power: Wazir, Mir Bakshi); Military assistance (military power: an attack on Hyderabad would no longer be an attack on the Mughal empire); Trade barriers (economic power: tariffs, while domestic trade is easier, cheaper, lower taxes).

Why it sticks: the four powers run Social, Political, Military, Economic, in the same order as S-A-M-T.

Memory hook

Nizam's two Delhi hats: 22-24 and 39-40.

Wazir at "twenty-two to twenty-four", Mir Bakshi in Nadir's time, "thirty-nine, forty".

Decode: Wazir 1722–24 (PM / Imperial Diwan) while still Subahdar of Hyderabad; Mir Bakshi 1739–40 (War Paymaster), again while Subahdar of Hyderabad. Nizam-ul-Mulk 24-48 is the man.

Why it sticks: the Wazir hat ends in 24, the very year Hyderabad is set up (1724).

3 · Mughal provincial titles and the Bengal Subah's structure

Memory hook

Sword, Purse, Eyes.

Three officers, three bosses at Delhi: the Sword answers to the Emperor, the Purse to the Wazir, the Eyes to the Mir Bakshi.

Decode: Subahdar (Nazim / Nawab) = Sword = Nizamat (defence, peace, criminal justice) reports to the Emperor; Diwan = Purse = Diwani (land revenue, civil justice) reports to the Imperial Diwan (Wazir), bypassing the Nazim; Bakshi of the Subah = Eyes (military audit and intelligence) reports to the Mir Bakshi, bypassing the Nazim. This is Akbar 56-05's checks and balances.

Why it sticks: nobody holds Sword, Purse and Eyes together, until a successor-State founder grabs all three.

Memory hook

Nazim and Nizam are anagrams.

Nazim = Subadar = Nawab = Governor; Naib = Deputy; "Nizam" is simply the Nazim's title in Hyderabad (same five letters, shuffled).

Decode: Alivardi Khan 40-56 was Nawab of Patna / Naib Nazim of Bihar first, and Subahdar of the whole Bengal Subah only in 1740, so a Nawab need not be a Subahdar.

Why it sticks: small seat (Patna) first, big seat (Bengal) later: deputy before boss.

3.1 Geographical administration of the Bengal Subah (handout tables)

Memory hook

Murshidabad is Mother, three Naib children.

Mother rules directly; the three Naib children rule by delegation: Patna-Bihar, Cuttack-Orissa, Dacca-East Bengal.

Decode: West Bengal = Murshidabad (Nazim and Diwan, direct rule); Bihar = Patna; Orissa = Cuttack; East Bengal = Dacca (each with a Naib Nazim and a Naib Diwan reporting to Murshidabad: delegated rule).

Why it sticks: say the pairs in table order; four seats, one direct, three delegated.

The Bakshi's four functions

Memory hook

Bakshi: Who, What-paid, Honest, Doing.

Four questions the Bakshi could always answer.

Decode: Who? = Mansabdari records; What paid? = military payroll (Jagirs transferred every 2-3 years); Honest? = Dagh (branded horse) and Chehra (soldier's face register); Doing? = intelligence (waqia-navis, daily reports to Delhi).

Why it sticks: Dagh is the mark on the horse's flank, Chehra is the face of the man.

4 · How all three successor States were built: the recipe

Memory hook

Tankha moves, Vatan stays.

Tankha = Transfer (temporary, shifted at will); Vatan = Vansh (hereditary, stays in the family).

Decode: Tankha Jagirs (temporary, transferable) versus Vatan Jagirs (hereditary). Converting Tankha into Vatan traded away the emperor's historic tool of control for the local loyalty of provincial Mansabdars.

Why it sticks: T for Transfer, V for Vansh (family line).

Memory hook

Every Eager Boss Can Convert.

E-E-B-C-C: the five-step recipe for a successor State.

Decode: Extract the Subahdari; End checks and balances (loyalists as Diwan, Naib Nazim, war commander); Build revenue strength (field survey, efficient LR system); Co-opt the men of money (merchants, bankers, Ijaradars given Mansabs); Convert Tankha Jagirs into Vatan Jagirs.

Why it sticks: result = a diffused autonomous polity. Chapter ruling on the count: the slide has five steps plus the result, the handout text says "four-step" and its diagram shows six; learn the recipe, not the number.

5 · Bengal: Murshid Quli Khan (MQK, 1717–27)

Memory hook

MQK: two hats, fifteen friends.

In '17 he wore two hats (Nazim and Diwan, Sword and Purse); fifteen friends (Zamindars) ate half the chapati (50% of LR) by '27.

Decode: MQK (Murshid Quli Khan, 1717–27) became Nazim in 1717 while already Diwan; by 1727 just 15 intermediary Zamindars collected 50% of Bengal Suba's LR; resisting Mansabdars had Tankha Jagirs shifted from Bengal to Orissa; he was the last Bengal Governor appointed as per the Emperor's wish.

Why it sticks: three numbers in a row, 17 (year), 15 (Zamindars), 50 (percent).

6 · The Jagat Seths: why Bengal's power was diffused, not centralised

Memory hook

Jagat Seth: M-M-S, the bank with three heirs.

Manikchand first (under MQK), then the pair Mehtab Rai and Swarup Chand (under Alivardi Khan 40-56).

Decode: Jagat Seth = hereditary title, not a name; a Jagat Seth was Treasurer of Bengal and Daroga of the Royal Mint under Shuja-ud-din Md Khan (1727–39). 1740 coup: Alivardi Khan 40-56 overthrew and killed Sarfaraz Khan (1739–40) with their backing and promised Md Shah one crore plus Sarfaraz's wealth.

Why it sticks: one bank, one title; Manik alone, then Mehtab-Swarup as a pair.

7 · Bengal's virtual break: the Maratha raids and Alivardi Khan 40-56

Memory hook

Raghuji's quarter and the Orissa key.

Raghuji Bhonsle of Nagpur raids for six years (45 to 51) and leaves with a quarter of two provinces and the whole third.

Decode: 1745-51 raids; 1751 settlement = Chauth (one-fourth) of Bengal and Bihar plus direct control of Orissa; then Alivardi Khan 40-56 stops sending revenue to Delhi and stops seeking approval for appointments.

Why it sticks: Bengal and Bihar pay (B-B), Orissa is gone (O).

The succession chain after Alivardi

Memory hook

Musical chairs in Murshidabad.

Many Sons-in-law Sit; Alivardi Seizes; Siraj Falls; Jafar, Qasim, Jafar swap the chair until the EIC takes the Diwani.

Decode: MQK (1717–27) → Shuja-ud-din (1727–39, son-in-law of MQK) → Sarfaraz Khan (1739–40) → Alivardi Khan 40-56 (coup) → Siraj-ud-Daula 56-57 → Mir Jafar 57-60 (1757-60, EIC puppet after Plassey) → Mir Qasim 60-63 (son-in-law of Mir Jafar 57-60) → Mir Jafar 57-60 restored (1763-65) → 1765 Diwani to the EIC.

Why it sticks: two sons-in-law bracket the chain (Shuja of MQK at the start, Qasim of Jafar near the end), and Jafar sits twice.

8 · Awadh: from Sadat Khan 22-39 to the virtual break of 1753

Memory hook

Three S's of Awadh.

Sadat, Safdar, Shuja: one gets the seat, one gets the Wazir's chair and has to quit, one is beaten at Buxar.

Decode: Sadat Khan 22-39 (Subahdar 1722, reward for crushing revolts) → nephew and son-in-law Safdar Jung 39-54 (Wazir under Ahmad Shah Bahadur in 1748; annexed Farukhabad; forced to resign 1753 = virtual break) → Shuja-ud-Daula 54-75 (Wazir under Shah Alam II 60-06; with Abdali at Panipat 1761; beaten by the EIC at Buxar 1764). Annexed 1856 from Wajid Ali Shah.

Why it sticks: the codes link end to start: 22-39, 39-54, 54-75.

Memory hook

Sadat felt "sad at" Delhi.

Passed over for Mir Bakshi, Sadat Khan 22-39 got Nadir Shah to invade Delhi in 1739, and ended ill-treated and dead by suicide.

Decode: the Mir Bakshi post went to Chin Qulich Khan (another name of Nizam-ul-Mulk 24-48) instead of him. Unlike MQK, Sadat Khan 22-39 kept his ambitions at the centre.

Why it sticks: "Sadat" sounds like "sad at" losing the post.

9 · Hyderabad: Nizam-ul-Mulk 24-48's path from Delhi to the Deccan

Memory hook

Nizam's number trail: 20, 22, 24, then 28, 38, then 39-40.

Twenty: Sayyids go. Twenty-two: Wazir. Twenty-four: Hyderabad. Twenty-eight and thirty-eight: Marathas win. Thirty-nine to forty: Mir Bakshi, then home for good.

Decode: 1720 Turani and Irani groups removed the Sayyid Brothers; Wazir 1722–24 (his Jagir redistribution failed); 1724 Hyderabad after defeating Mubariz Khan; Marathas defeated him in 1728 and 1738 (Chauth and Sardeshmukhi); Mir Bakshi 1739–40; by 1740 he moved to Hyderabad permanently. One man, three names: Chin Qulich Khan = Nizam-ul-Mulk 24-48 = Asaf Jah I.

Why it sticks: an even run 20-22-24, a pair 28-38, then 39-40.

Memory hook

Never Make Salabat Nervous.

N-M-S-N: the four heirs after Nizam-ul-Mulk 24-48.

Decode: Nazir Jung (1748–50) → Muzaffar Jung 50-51 (1750–51, only 3 months) → Salabat Jung 51-62 (French dominated under Bussy) → Nizam-Ali-Khan (1762–1803).

Why it sticks: the dates chain 48-50-51-62, and the shortest reign is the second one (3 months).

Memory hook

A-H = I-T.

Alphabet match: Awadh then Hyderabad = Irani then Turani.

Decode: Sadat Khan 22-39 of Awadh = "of Irani group" (the handout's Karnal passage, flagged in the chapter's note); Nizam-ul-Mulk 24-48 of Hyderabad = leader of the Turani group.

Why it sticks: A comes before H, and I comes before T.

10 · How Nizam established Hyderabad as a successor State

Memory hook

Name, Peace, Upstarts.

Hyderabad's three marks: the emperor's Name on coins and Khutba, Peace through tolerance, Upstarts as Mansabdars.

Decode: Name = symbolic sovereignty (coins, Friday Khutba, zero influence over war, peace, treaties, appointments); Peace = tolerance (Hindu majority economically powerful; opponents punished regardless of community); Upstarts = merchants, moneylenders and Ijaradars made Mansabdars on the largest scale, with Tankha to Vatan converted most aggressively.

Why it sticks: N-P-U, "Nizam's Peaceful Upstarts".

11.1 Backstory: the Vijayanagar Empire (1336–1646)

Memory hook

E-N-P: Empire, Nayak, Poligar.

Emperor on top, Nayak (the CM) in the middle, Poligar (a fort-owning local chief) at the bottom.

Decode: Empire (Vijayanagar, 1336–1646, strongest in South India for about 300 years) → provinces under Nayaks (Telugu Hindu governors from warrior families; teacher: Nayak = CM) → Palayams under a Poligar (Palayakkarar) with his own private army and fort.

Why it sticks: three rungs, E-N-P, read top to bottom.

11.2 The long decline (1565–1698)

Memory hook

Big Aunt Gave Bananas.

B-A-G-B: the four Deccan Sultanates that broke Vijayanagar at Talikota in 1565.

Decode: Bijapur, Ahmadnagar, Golconda, Bidar. Talikota (1565) was the real end of Vijayanagar power, though the empire officially continued until 1646.

Why it sticks: four bananas, four Sultanates.

Memory hook

MTG: Mind The Gap.

The southern Nayaks of Madurai, Tanjore and Gingee ruled autonomously after 1565 because distance protected them, until Bijapur and Golconda divided the Carnatic by 1646.

Decode: M = Madurai, T = Tanjore, G = Gingee.

Why it sticks: the "gap" is the distance that kept them safe for a while.

Memory hook

Gingee's years: 77, 89-90, 98.

Shivaji 74-80 grabs it in 77, Rajaram 89-00 makes it his base in 89, the siege starts in 90 and lasts 8 years, so it falls in 98.

Decode: Shivaji 74-80 captured Gingee from Bijapur (1677); after Sambhaji 80-89 was executed (1689), Rajaram 89-00 made it his base; Zulfiqar Khan (Nawab of Carnatic) besieged it from 1690 for eight years, the Mughals' longest siege; captured 1698. Aurangzeb 58-07 had taken Bijapur (1686) and Golconda (1687).

Why it sticks: 1690 + 8 = 1698; and Bijapur (86) falls before Golconda (87), B before G.

11.3 Dividing the region

Memory hook

Carnatic = the tenant downstairs.

Hyderabad Suba is a two-floor house: the Nizam lives upstairs (north of the Krishna); the Carnatic tenant lives downstairs (south of it) with his own Nawab, but he reports to the Nizam.

Decode: Hyderabad = north of the Krishna, ruled directly by the Nizam; Carnatic (Hyderabad-Karnatic) = twelve districts south of the Krishna, capital Gingee, own Nawab, a "state within a state". Prelims 2021 trap: the Carnatic was not a separate independent State; it was the southern part of the Hyderabad Subah.

Why it sticks: a tenant lives inside the landlord's building, never in a separate one. Teacher's parallels: J&K (Art. 370), Meghalaya within Assam (Art. 244A).

11.4 and 11.5 Towards autonomy: the Carnatic Nawabs

Memory hook

S-D-A: Saadat shifts, Dost dies, Anwar is the Nizam's pick.

Saadatullah (10-32) shifts the capital to Arcot and makes the post hereditary; Dost (32-40) is killed in Chittoor; Anwar (from 44) comes in on the Nizam's choice but goes autonomous too.

Decode: Saadatullah Khan (1710–32): last Nawab chosen by the emperor; moved the capital from Gingee to Arcot; adopted his brother Ghulam Ali's son Dost Ali. Dost Ali Khan (1732–40): killed in 1740 by the Marathas under Raghuji Bhonsle in Chittoor district. Anwar-ud-din Khan (from 1744): chosen by the Nizam.

Why it sticks: each start year equals the previous end year (32, then 40), then 44; and Gingee to Arcot goes G then A.

Memory hook

Ambur 49, Wallajah 52.

Anwar falls at Ambur in 49; Wallajah rises in 52 with British backing.

Decode: Anwar-ud-din killed at the Battle of Ambur (1749), 2nd Carnatic War, French versus British; Md Ali 52-95 Wallajah (1752–95), British-backed, leading to dependency on the EIC.

Why it sticks: three years from Ambur (49) to Wallajah (52): one Nawab ends, the next begins.

12 · Synthesis: the common pattern and what it made possible

Memory hook

57, 64, 98: the EIC knocks in B-A-H order.

Bengal falls at 57, Awadh at 64, Hyderabad's slot says 98.

Decode: the teacher's EIC column against Bengal, Awadh, Hyderabad = 1757 (Plassey), 1764 (Buxar), 1798. The chapter notes that the slides and handout do not explain 1798, so check it against your own notes.

Why it sticks: 57 to 64 is seven years; then the odd one out, 98.

Memory hook

Half to save, full to seize.

The Nizam offered Nadir Rs 50 lakh to save Delhi (an attempt); Alivardi offered the emperor a full crore to seize Bengal.

Decode: Nizam as Mir Bakshi bought Nadir off with Rs 50 lakh (attempt); Alivardi Khan 40-56 promised Md Shah one crore plus Sarfaraz's personal wealth, and approval was given.

Why it sticks: 50 lakh is only half a crore; half saves nothing, full buys a crown.

Memory hook

Break order: 40, 51, 53.

Hyderabad drifts away first (after 1740), Bengal breaks after the 1751 settlement, Awadh breaks in 1753.

Decode: Hyderabad = after 1740 the Nizam moves there for good; Bengal = Alivardi Khan 40-56 after the settlement with Raghuji Bhonsle (1751); Awadh = 1753, Safdar Jung 39-54 forced to resign.

Why it sticks: always ascending, 40, then 51, then 53, in the order H, B, A.

Memory hooks: chapter in one breath

Memory hook

One breath: from Delhi's decline to the EIC.

In '17 MQK (1717–27) wears two hats in Bengal; in '22 Sadat Khan 22-39 is rewarded with Awadh; in '24 Nizam-ul-Mulk 24-48 takes Hyderabad: "BAH!", yet none says "independent", because the emperor's name still keeps trade easy and enemies divided (S-A-M-T).

Five steps (E-E-B-C-C) build each State, and the Sword, Purse and Eyes end up in one hand.

Bengal: the Jagat Seths' money helps Alivardi Khan 40-56 win in '40, and Raghuji's quarter plus Orissa in '51 completes the break.

Awadh: Sadat, Safdar, Shuja; Safdar Jung 39-54 resigns in '53.

Hyderabad: Nizam-ul-Mulk 24-48 goes home for good in '40, with the Carnatic as the tenant downstairs.

Then the EIC steps into the gap between paper and power: Plassey 57, Buxar 64, and the teacher's 98.

Chapter 4: The Marathas: Rise, Administration & the Road to Panipat 1761 (48)open chapter

1.1 Who he was

Memory hook

Sixteen in sixteen-forty-six.

He took his first fort at 16, in 16-46: the "16" shows up twice.

Decode: born 19 February 1630 to Shahji Bhonsle (Jagirdar of Poona, General and Mansabdar in Bijapur) and Jijabai (the deeper inspiration); trained by guardian Dadaji Kondadev ("Dada" = elder guardian); first fort 1646; base = Maval region in the Sahyadri.

Why it sticks: 1630 + 16 = 1646, so age and year match.

1.3 Why he rose against Bijapur and the Mughals

Memory hook

Shivaji 74-80's chain: Fort, Fight, Fangs; Surat, Siege, Sadhu; Crown, Close.

46-57-59 = Fort, Fight, Fangs; 63-65-66 = Surat, Siege, Sadhu; 74-80 = Crown, Close.

Decode: 1646 first fort at 16; 1657 systematic attacks on Bijapur begin; 1659 Afzal Khan killed with Bagh Nakh (tiger claws) and Bichhwa; 1663 Shaista Khan 64-88 defeated and Surat sacked; 1665 Jai Singh 99-43 besieges Purandhar; 1666 Agra imprisonment and the Sadhu escape; 1674 coronation at Raigarh; 1680 natural death (Shivaji 74-80).

Why it sticks: three triples of alliterating words, then the two ends of his reign.

1.4 Treaty of Purandhar 1665: a tactical pause, not a permanent defeat

Memory hook

Fat Elephants Squash Bananas.

F-E-S-B: the four terms of the Treaty of Purandhar (1665).

Decode: Forts (23 of 35 surrendered, so 12 kept); Emperor (Aurangzeb 58-07 accepted, but no personal mansab for Shivaji 74-80); Sambhaji 80-89 made a Mughal Mansabdar of 5,000; Bijapur (support the Mughals against it). Jai Singh 99-43 forced it by a gherao (starving siege).

Why it sticks: "two-three of three-five" = 23 of 35. Teacher's ruling: a temporary tactic; 1665 bows, 1674 crowns (the coronation was its legal reversal). Treaties are named after the place where signed.

2.1 The army: light cavalry and guerrilla warfare

Memory hook

Light Lives off the land; Heavy Hauls its kitchen.

L-L and H-H: light cavalry = horses only, food and shelter on the way (by love or force); heavy cavalry = elephants plus supply carriers, slow.

Decode: Shivaji 74-80's army = about 30,000 to 40,000 light cavalry and 240 forts (many in the Konkan); the Mughals relied on heavy cavalry. Speed is the focus of guerrilla warfare.

Why it sticks: two numbers, two lines: "240 forts, 30-40 thousand horses".

2.2 The Saranjami system: military land grants

Memory hook

Zero, One, Never.

Shivaji 74-80 gave zero Jagirs; Shahu 19-49 gave one kind (Tankha, i.e. Saranjam); and never the other kind (Vatan).

Decode: Shivaji 74-80 never gave Jagirs (cash pay, centralised); Shahu 19-49 gave Tankha Jagirs = Saranjam to military commanders (temporary, tied to service, not heritable) but never Vatan Jagirs. Vatan (hereditary) belongs to civil officials: Patils and Deshmukhs.

Why it sticks: Saranjam = Soldier, Service-bound; Vatan = Vansh (family line), civil.

2.3 The navy: Father of the Modern Indian Navy

Memory hook

20, 200, 700: zeros trail.

20 ships in '57, 200 by '64, a peak of 700: ten times in seven years, then more.

Decode: Shivaji 74-80's navy = 20 ships (1657), full navy of 200 (1664), peak Maratha navy 700; asymmetric coastal defence, not deep-ocean war. 1668: French factory at Rajapur in return for Cannons, Lead, Saltpetre (C-L-S). Crew: Kolis and Bhandaris; commanders Mayanak Bhandari and Daulat Khan; all indigenous, hence "Father of the Modern Indian Navy".

Why it sticks: 1664 - 1657 = 7 years for the tenfold jump; C-L-S = the three supplies he secured.

2.4 The fleet: tactical ship design

Memory hook

Ghurab Guns, Galbat Gallops.

Ghurab = gunboat; Galbat = fast rowboat that gallops ahead or tows; Shibars and Pals = the big ships.

Decode: Ghurabs: gunboats with heavy artillery; Galbats: fast, manoeuvrable rowboats with light sails that tow Ghurabs in dead winds or chase fleeing ships; Shibars and Pals: larger vessels for transport and heavier engagements. Shipyards used Sahyadri teak.

Why it sticks: G-G pairs the two small, fast partners (gun and tow); the big pair comes last.

2.5 The forts: the Jaladurg doctrine

Memory hook

S-V-S: Shivaji 74-80's Very Strong sea forts.

Sindhudurg, Vijaydurg, Suvarnadurg: the three named Jaladurg examples.

Decode: Jaladurg doctrine = sea forts on rocky islands or peninsulas; they sheltered docked ships and their cannons kept enemy ships away. Teacher's Bombay chain: 1668 EIC already in Bombay; 1687 Bombay Presidency (a group of seven islands), because Surat feared Maratha cavalry.

Why it sticks: three S-V-S forts; and "68 in, 87 Presidency".

Memory hook

12 forts = 4 chunks of 3.

Lo-Kha-Rai, Su-Vi-Sin, Sal-Shiv-Raj, Pra-Pan-Gin: four beats of three.

Decode: Lohagad, Khanderi, Raigad | Suvarnadurg, Vijaydurg, Sindhudurg | Salher, Shivneri, Rajgad | Pratapgad, Panhala, Gingee (the map's order); Gingee (Jinji, in the Carnatic) closes the list.

Why it sticks: the second chunk is the three sea-forts (S-V-S) you already know.

2.6 After Shivaji 74-80: Admiral Kanhoji Angre

Memory hook

Kanhoji: 14-24, dies 29; navy dies 56, Plassey 57.

Five foes: Big Pigs Don't Swim Slowly = British, Portuguese, Dutch, Sawants, Siddis.

Decode: Admiral Kanhoji Angre, the "Shivaji 74-80 of the Seas", dominated the Arabian Sea for about 30 years until his death in 1729; raided the EIC at Bombay 1714-24 (stalemate); the British defeated the Maratha navy in 1756, one year before Plassey (1757). He switched from Tarabai to Shahu 19-49 in 1714.

Why it sticks: the 14 repeats (switch in 1714, raids from 1714) and 56 sits right before 57.

3 · Sambhaji 80-89 and Rajaram 89-00: the troubled succession

Memory hook

Shivaji 74-80's family chain: S-S-R-T-S.

"Sweet Sons Rule; Tarabai Stops; Shahu 19-49 returns."

Decode: Shivaji 74-80 (natural death) → elder son Sambhaji 80-89 (captured, tortured, assassinated by Aurangzeb 58-07) → younger son Rajaram 89-00 (TB at 30) → widow Tarabai, regent for infant Shivaji II (1700-07) → 1707 the Mughals release Shahu 19-49 (elder son of Sambhaji 80-89) → civil war to 1719.

Why it sticks: each code ends where the next starts (80, 89, 00); and 7 years of regency + 12 years of civil war = 19, the year Shahu 19-49 starts. Elder dies by torture, younger by illness.

4.2 The eight ministers

Memory hook

Walk through the durbar.

Peshwa on the throne (PM), Amatya at the cashbox (finance), Sachiv at the letter desk (correspondence), Sumanta at the door with envoys (foreign affairs), Senapati at the sword rack (military), Panditrao at the temple corner (religious affairs), Nyayadhish at the scales (justice), Waqenavis behind the curtain (intelligence).

Decode: the eight Ashtapradhan in table order: Peshwa, Amatya, Sachiv, Sumanta, Senapati, Panditrao, Nyayadhish, Waqenavis (P-A-S-S-S-P-N-W).

Why it sticks: walk the room in one direction, throne first and spy last.

4.3 The Peshwa's own council: Darakhdars and the Barbhai Mandal

Memory hook

Bar-bhai: 12 Bros in '74.

1674 crown, 1774 Twelve: exactly a century apart.

Decode: Barbhai Mandal (Council of Twelve, "12 Bros") formed in 1774 by twelve Darakhdars (the Peshwa's own senior civil servants) under Nana Fadnavis; the Hazur Daftar = the Peshwa's secretariat at Poona, about 200 staff. Shivaji 74-80 was crowned in 1674.

Why it sticks: the "74" repeats exactly 100 years apart (1674, 1774).

4.6 Sources of revenue

Memory hook

L-C-J-P, and who pays Nazrana.

Land first, then Customs and octroi, Judicial, Permits.

Decode: LR (by quality of soil, irrigation, crop sown); customs duty and octroi (octroi = local trade, customs = international: "Octroi = Own town, Customs = Countries"); judicial proceeds (Nazrana paid by the winning party, Jurmana by the losing party); fees for permits and licences (wood cutting, drugs, private minting).

Why it sticks: Nazrana = Nice win (winner pays); Jurmana = Judged against (loser pays).

5 · Shahu 19-49's release (1707): was the Mughal "pacifist policy" really pacifist?

Memory hook

Mughals ruled by ROD.

Reconciliation, Opportunism, Divide-and-rule: pragmatic real-politik, not pacifism.

Decode: after Aurangzeb 58-07, R-O-D = reconciliation plus opportunism plus divide-and-rule, "nominal pacifism with plausible deniability". Teacher: why release Shahu 19-49? To keep better relations with a rival, and because a Maratha civil war (1707-19) would weaken the enemy from within.

Why it sticks: a ROD in the hand, not a dove on the flag.

Memory hook

07 gift, 08 grave.

1707: a mansab and a promise of Anandpur; 1708: assassinated at Nanded.

Decode: Bahadur Shah (1707-12) gave Guru Gobind Singh 76-08 a high Mansab and promised Anandpur; Wazir Khan, Faujdar of Sirhind, had him assassinated in 1708 at Nanded, Maharashtra; no action was taken and Anandpur was never handed over. Handout note: Sirhind = a Sarkar (district) in the Lahore Suba; a Faujdar = military, judicial and land-revenue functions over a district, not a Governor.

Why it sticks: the gift and the grave are one year apart.

6.2 Treaty of Warna 1731: the family settlement

Memory hook

Warna 1731: two thrones from one family.

Satara for Shahu 19-49 (S for S); Kolhapur for the second wife's son, Sambhaji II ("second son, second seat").

Decode: Treaty of Warna (1731): Shahu 19-49 gave Kolhapur to Sambhaji II, son of Rajaram 89-00 by his second wife Rajasbai (in control since about 1714); two Chhatrapati dynasties, Satara and Kolhapur. Later: Ramraja (Rajaram II) 1749-77 ceremonial; Satara ended with EIC annexation in 1848; Kolhapur merged in 1947.

Why it sticks: one treaty, one split house: Satara and Kolhapur.

7 · Why Shahu 19-49 won, and who the Maratha Sardars were

Memory hook

TNT with a GLIM.

TNT = Technology, Numbers (army size / alliances), Tactics; GLIM = Geography, Leadership, Infrastructure (forts), Money.

Decode: the teacher's seven reasons for victory in any war: technology, leadership, tactics, geography, money, number of soldiers or size of army (alliances), military infrastructure (forts).

Why it sticks: an explosion (TNT) plus a small light (GLIM): seven letters, seven reasons.

7.2 Who were the Maratha Sardars?

Memory hook

BIGN: four big names, four capitals.

Learn the Sardars in table order, G-H-S-B, and the capitals spell BIGN.

Decode: Gaikwad = Baroda, Holkar = Indore, Scindia (Shinde) = Gwalior, Bhonsle = Nagpur; capitals in the same order = B, I, G, N.

Why it sticks: "BIG N(ame)": the first letters of the capitals form a word, so the order cannot slip.

8.1 Svarajya versus Samrajya

Memory hook

Shiv-Raigarh, Raja-Jinji, Shahu-Satara.

Three kings, three capitals, matched by first letters: Raigarh, Jinji, Satara.

Decode: Shivaji 74-80 → Raigarh (1647-1689); Rajaram 89-00 → Jinji (1689-1698); Shahu 19-49 → Satara; Peshwa at Poona. The chapter flags a handout conflict (the handout ties Satara to Rajaram 89-00 and to Svarajya's headquarters); follow the teacher's chain Raigarh, Jinji, Satara.

Why it sticks: Jinji is the Carnatic fort, the odd one out, and sits in the middle.

8.3 Sardeshmukhi: an additional 10%, internal and external

Memory hook

Chauth = Chaar, one door; Sardeshmukhi = two doors.

Chauth is a quarter (25%), collected only outside; Sardeshmukhi is an extra 10%, collected inside and outside; together 25 + 10 = 35.

Decode: Chauth = one-fourth of revenue paid by a protected state for military protection (external only; a tool of empire-building); Sardeshmukhi = additional 10% (Deshmukhs deposit it to the Chhatrapati; Shivaji 74-80 declared himself Sardeshmukh), internal and external.

Why it sticks: "Chauth - Chaar (four) - quarter"; Chauth has one door (outside), Sardeshmukhi has two.

8.4 The teacher's boards: how Chauth and Sardeshmukhi work

Memory hook

100, 10, 90, 1, 91.

A Deshmukh collects 100, keeps 10, hands over 90, and pays 1 (10% of his 10) as Sardeshmukhi: the Chhatrapati's side gets 91.

Decode: Rs 100 collected; commission Rs 10; Rs 90 to the state; Sardeshmukhi = 10% of Rs 10 = Rs 1; 90 + 1 = Rs 91. Chapter ruling: the table says "extra 10% of land revenue", the teacher's internal example applies it to the Deshmukh's commission; learn the table wording and this example for the internal case. External: Chauth + Sardeshmukhi = 25% + 10% = 35%.

Why it sticks: "ten percent of ten is one".

8.5 How Chauth was divided

Memory hook

66-25-6-3: Mokasa Mine, Babti Boss, Sahotra Secretary, Nadgaunda Nice.

The biggest slice goes to whoever has the troops; the next to the king; then the secretary; the smallest to charity.

Decode: Mokasa 66% kept by the Sardars for troops; Babti 25% to the Chhatrapati; Sahotra 6% to the Pant Sachiv; Nadgaunda 3% at the Chhatrapati's discretion. 66 + 25 + 6 + 3 = 100. The Sardar keeps two-thirds and one-third goes to Svarajya (teacher: the Chhatrapati gets 1/3).

Why it sticks: M-B-S-N and sizes that fall in order, 66, 25, 6, 3.

8.6 Chauth as a tool of empire-building: the teacher's boards

Memory hook

25, 35, 60, ALL.

The treaty ladder: pay a quarter, pay 35, pay 60, or lose the whole land.

Decode: 1 Chauth (25%); 2 Chauth + Sardeshmukhi (25 + 10 = 35%); 3 Chauth x 2 + Sardeshmukhi (25 x 2 + 10 = 60%; 1727 Gujarat paid 60%); 4 annexation of territory. The bigger the power gap between winner and loser, the harsher the treaty.

Why it sticks: the numbers climb 25, 35, 60 and then everything.

Memory hook

The boiling frog.

A short war is warm water; Chauth is the heat turned up every year; after about ten years the frog (the external state) cannot jump, and the Marathas simply take it.

Decode: short war, then a peace treaty with Chauth (25% of annual revenue); the drain (less investment, less spent on the army, revenue falls, e.g. Rs 90 instead of Rs 100, yet 25% is still due); after ten years a weak economy and weak army, unable to pay; short war, easy total victory, annexation. Direct annexation (Scenario 1) = long, expensive, high loss of men.

Why it sticks: slow heat is cheaper than a long fight.

8.7 How the Peshwa office became hereditary; the power shift and Sangola 1750

Memory hook

Three in a row makes a custom.

Father, son, grandson: Balaji Vishwanath 13-20, Baji Rao 20-40, Nana Saheb (1740-61): three generations, so the office is hereditary in effect.

Decode: the Peshwa's office was originally not hereditary; Balaji Vishwanath 13-20 (sixth Peshwa) changed it in practice. Sangola (February 1750): Nana Saheb backed Ramraja (Tarabai denied he was her grandson), who signed away all real power to the Peshwa.

Why it sticks: three Peshwas, three generations, then Sangola 1750 seals it.

8.8 The series of Peshwas

Memory hook

Eight Peshwas in a rhyme.

Balaji began it, Baji ran wide,

Nana lost Panipat, Madhav had pride,

Narayan was murdered, Raghunath grabbed,

Madhav-Two infant, Baji-Two last.

Decode: Balaji Vishwanath 13-20 (sixth Peshwa, hereditary in practice); Baji Rao 20-40 (Delhi plundered 1737, Treaty of Bhopal 1738); Nana Saheb (1740-61) (Panipat era, Sangola 1750); Madhav Rao 61-72 (young but able, natural death 1772); Narayan Rao 72-73 (murdered 1773 by his uncle); Raghunath Rao 73-74 (usurper); Madhav Rao II 74-95 (infant, regent Nana Fadnavis, suicide 1795); Baji Rao II 96-18 (last, son of Raghunath Rao 73-74).

Why it sticks: 72, 73, 74 are three consecutive years, each opening a new seat (Narayan, Raghunath, Madhav II). Note: the chapter's table writes Nana Saheb's code as 20-40 in one place; the slides and the (1740-61) form are used here.

8.9 The three broad phases of Maratha polity after Shivaji 74-80

Memory hook

T-G-D: Turmoil, Growth, Decline.

80, 19, 61 are the hinges.

Decode: Phase 1, 1680-1719, turmoil (Mughal warfare after Shivaji 74-80's death, civil war 1707-19); Phase 2, 1719-1761, growth and expansion; Phase 3, 1761 onwards, decline, with a revival in the 1770s (First Anglo-Maratha War 1775-82).

Why it sticks: 1719 is the year Shahu 19-49 starts and 1761 is Panipat: "Tough Growth Declines".

9.1 The 1719 turning point: alliance with the Sayyid Brothers

Memory hook

1719 deal: A-C-S.

One right (Autonomy), one pair (Chauth of two), one half-dozen (Six provinces).

Decode: Peshwa Balaji Vishwanath 13-20 with the Sayyid Brothers removed Farrukhsiyar 13-19 and made Muhammad Shah 19-48 a puppet. Gains: (A) recognised autonomy in Maharashtra; (C) Chauth of Malwa and Gujarat; (S) Chauth and Sardeshmukhi from six Deccan provinces. Reversal: 1720 the Nizam defeated the Sayyids; 1724 he refused to pay.

Why it sticks: the teacher boxed these gains for Prelims.

Memory hook

Kind Hyderabadi Aunties Bring Big Biryani.

K-H-A-B-B-B: the six Deccan provinces.

Decode: Khandesh, Hyderabad, Aurangabad, Berar, Bidar, Bijapur (some sources name Golconda as the sixth, since Bijapur later merged into Golconda); the Nizam's Hyderabad State was later built from exactly these six.

Why it sticks: the last three all start with B.

9.2 The Maratha decade of expansion (1727-39)

Memory hook

27-28-29: Gujarat, Deccan, Malwa.

"Good Deccan Mangoes": G-D-M for the first three expansions.

Decode: 1727-28 Gujarat (60% of revenue as annual tribute); 1728 Hyderabad (Chauth and Sardeshmukhi of the Deccan regained); 1729 Malwa (reach into Rajasthan); 1731 a joint attack by the Nizam and Gujarat chiefs defeated.

Why it sticks: the years run 27, 28, 29 in a row, and 31 is the single repeat attack.

Memory hook

36-37-38-39: Konkan, Delhi, Bhopal, Bassein.

Four years, four steps: "Kings Demand Big Bounties".

Decode: 1736 Portuguese and Ethiopian Muslims removed from the Konkan coast; 1737 Baji Rao 20-40 plundered Delhi and briefly held Muhammad Shah 19-48 prisoner; 1738 Treaty of Bhopal; 1739 Treaty of Bassein with the Portuguese, annexation of Bassein (Vasai).

Why it sticks: K-D-B-B climbs one step each year, 36 to 39.

Memory hook

Bhopal 1738: Malwa is the filling between Chambal and Narmada.

Chambal on top, Narmada below, Malwa in the sandwich.

Decode: after beating a large Mughal army commanded by the Nizam, the Marathas got the Suba of Malwa (held de facto since 1729) and sovereignty over all lands between the rivers Chambal and Narmada.

Why it sticks: the teacher's sketch shows Chambal above, the Malwa oval, Narmada below.

9.3 Expansion continues on many fronts (1745-54)

Memory hook

48, 51, 52, 54: Manupur, Bhalke, Ahmedabad, Alamgir.

Sikhs help stall Abdali at Manupur in 48; Bhalke costs Hyderabad Khandesh in 51; Ahmedabad completes Gujarat in 52; Alamgir the puppet in 54.

Decode: 1748 first Abdali invasion checked at Manupur (Mughal pitched battle, Sikh misls harassed his supply lines); 1751 Treaty of Bhalke, Salabat Jung 51-62 ceded Khandesh; 1752 Ahmedabad taken, Gujarat annexed, Damaji Gaikwad Governor, HQ Baroda; 1754 Alamgir II 54-59 installed as puppet. Also 1745-51 Raghuji Bhonsle took Odisha and the Chauth of Bengal and Bihar from Alivardi Khan 40-56.

Why it sticks: M-B-A-A in year order, 48, 51, 52, 54.

10.1 Sides and background

Memory hook

Sixty beats fifty: A-R-B-A.

Abdali, Rohillas, Bangash, Awadh: the four partners against Bhau's 50,000.

Decode: Marathas (about 50,000) under Sadasiv Rao Bhau versus an alliance (about 60,000) of Ahmad Shah Abdali 47-72, the Rohillas under Najib-ud-Daula 57-70 (Rohilkhand), the Bangash Pathans under Ahmad Khan (1750-82) (Farrukhabad) and Awadh under Shuja-ud-Daula 54-75.

Why it sticks: four partners in A-R-B-A; the bigger number (60) won.

Memory hook

Abdali clock: 47, 56, 58, 61.

"'47 a state he made; '56 Lahore he took; '58 his son was shooed; '61 the Marathas shook."
