Decode: Satara annexed in 1848 under Dalhousie 48-56's Doctrine of Lapse. The year and the first number of his code match (48). Satara and Kolhapur had been spared in 1818 as the Chhatrapati's symbolic line.

Why it sticks: "48 and 48": the day he arrived, the Chhatrapati's Satara lapsed.

8 · Why did the Marathas lose?

Memory hook

F-U-F-N: Factions, Unity, Funds, Navy.

Decode: four reasons the Marathas lost: Factionalism, no Unified command (Scindhia, Holkar, Bhonsle, Gaikwad of Baroda), the EIC's Finances, the EIC's Naval and logistics edge (Konkan coast blockaded; sea and roads).

Why it sticks: "Fun!" with a missing "N": two internal, two external; the Marathas had no fun.

9 · Wars due to the EIC army over-hyping the threat to borders

Memory hook

Trade, then T: two branches, two drivers.

Decode: Wars by the EIC = for trade or for T (territory). For territory: empire-builder GGs (Wellesley 98-05, Francis Hastings 13-23) or the army's vested interest (it overhypes the threat to the borders).

Why it sticks: a tree has one trunk and one fork: the army branch is the one that overhypes.

Memory hook

Protectorate: Sign kept, Home kept, Foreign lost.

Decode: protectorate = symbolic sovereignty kept, domestic functions kept, external functions go to Britain. (Same logic as a Subsidiary Alliance.)

Why it sticks: you keep your signboard and your house, but someone else answers the doorbell for foreign visitors.

9.1 Nepal, Burma and Coorg

Memory hook

Sugauli 1816: Nepal gives K-G-T.

Decode: Treaty of Sugauli (1816), after the Anglo-Nepal War (1814-16, Francis Hastings 13-23): Nepal a British Protectorate; it loses Kumaon, Garhwal and the Terai.

Why it sticks: "KGT" sounds like "Kya Gaya? Terai!"; and 14-16 is just the war's span.

Memory hook

Yandabo 1826: A-N-A-T plus Cachar 1830.

Decode: Treaty of Yandabo (1826), Amherst 23-28: annexed Assam, Nagaland, Arakan and Tenasserim (parts of lower Burma); Cachar annexed 1830. The Ahom Kingdom of Assam, which had backed the EIC, was stabbed in the back.

Why it sticks: "ANAT" like "Anaath": Assam, abandoned by its ally; 1824-26 war, 1826 treaty, 1830 Cachar.

Memory hook

Coorg 1834: coffee after the king.

Decode: Coorg (1834), William Bentinck 28-35: annexed on grounds of maladministration by Vira Raja; massive coffee plantations were set up afterwards.

Why it sticks: the cup of coffee in your hand grew on an annexed hill.

9.2 Afghanistan and Sindh

Memory hook

Afghan war: 20,000, Rs 1.5 crore, 1 survivor.

Decode: 1st Anglo-Afghan War (1838-42), Auckland 36-42's Forward Policy: Shah Shuja against Dost Mohammed; 20,000 dead, Rs 1.5 crore, one European survivor of the retreat from Kabul; Auckland 36-42 recalled, Ellenborough 42-44 ended it; Dost Mohammed stayed friendly with the stronger British.

Why it sticks: 2, 1.5, 1: a falling count that ends in one man at the gate.

Memory hook

Sindh 1843: Char Yaar, Talpurs, Napier's "Peccavi".

Decode: Sindh annexed 1843 by Ellenborough 42-44 from the Char Yaar (four Baloch chiefs of the Talpur dynasty) without justification; commander Sir Charles Napier, whose one-word dispatch Peccavi means "I have sinned" and puns "I have Sindh".

Why it sticks: four friends (Char Yaar), one pun; Sindh and "sinned" sound the same.

Memory hook

Border wars in order: N-B-C-A-S-S-B, 14-24-34-38-43-45-52.

Decode: Nepal 1814-16 (Francis Hastings 13-23), Burma I 1824-26 (Amherst 23-28), Coorg 1834 (William Bentinck 28-35), Afghan 1838-42 (Auckland 36-42), Sindh 1843 (Ellenborough 42-44), Sikh 1845-46 and 1848-49 (Hardinge 44-48 and Dalhousie 48-56), Burma II 1852-53 (Dalhousie 48-56).

Why it sticks: "Nine Boys Cook, Always Serve Sweet Bread": the first three starts are 14, 24, 34 (ten-year steps), then 38, 43, 45, 52.

Memory hook

Governors General in the same order: F-A-B-A-E-H-D.

Decode: Francis Hastings 13-23, Amherst 23-28, William Bentinck 28-35, Auckland 36-42, Ellenborough 42-44, Hardinge 44-48, Dalhousie 48-56. Read the codes in a row: 13-23, 23-28, 28-35, 36-42, 42-44, 44-48, 48-56; each starts where (or just after) the last one ended.

Why it sticks: "FAB" then "A-E-H-D": a fab team passing the baton year by year.

10 · The Prelims PYQs on the class slides (slides 88 to 94)

Memory hook

Prelims 1999: 75, 80, 24, 38 = 3, 4, 1, 2.

Decode: 1775 First Anglo-Maratha (3), 1780 Second Anglo-Mysore (4), 1824 First Anglo-Burmese (1), 1838 First Anglo-Afghan (2). Answer (c). Use the start year.

Why it sticks: the matching digits read 3-4-1-2, "three-four, one-two", a counting rhyme.

Memory hook

Prelims 2004: only the middle two are true.

Decode: A 1767-69 = Mysore I, not Maratha I (that is 1775); B 1790-92 = Mysore III (true); C 1824-26 = Burmese I (true); D 1845-46 = Sikh I, not Sikh II (1848-49). Answer (d) 2 and 3.

Why it sticks: the two ends lie, the middle two (B, C = 2, 3) are honest.

Memory hook

Prelims 2010: Lahore, Timur Shah, 1761.

Decode: Abdali's immediate reason for the Third Panipat (1761): the Marathas expelled his viceroy (his son) Timur Shah from Lahore. Not Adina Beg's invitation (he invited the Marathas), not tribute, not a general annexation plan.

Why it sticks: "Timur's Tale ends at Lahore": one trigger, one year on the board.

Memory hooks: chapter in one breath

Memory hook

The story of "The Dozen, the Dagger and the Divided House".

A dozen men (Nana Fadnis and eleven) depose Raghunath Rao 73-74, who mortgages Salsette and Bassein at Surat (1775); Warren Hastings 73-85 signs Purandhar (1776), Bombay hides the claimant, Wadgaon (1779) goes to N-M-T, and Salbai (1782) buys peace with an alliance.

The glue goes: 94 dies, 95 suicides, 96 crowned, 1800 Nana Fadnis gone.

Vithoji's Vengeance Brings Bassein (1802): Gaikwad first, then the Peshwa, then Scindhia; Arthur's toughest fight at Assaye (1803); Wellesley 98-05 is recalled and Cornwallis 86-93 dies in a quarter-year.

Francis Hastings 13-23 crushes the sacked Pindaris and runs Paramountcy 13-58; Appa Sahib's loophole at Nagpur (1816) roars at Sitabuldi (Nov 1817); S-M-K-S: Sitabuldi, Mahidpur, Koregaon, Surrender (June 1818).

Pension, Swallow, Oust, Mandsaur, Spare: the Peshwaship ends; Satara falls in 1848.

Then the border wars march: Nepal 14, Burma 24, Coorg 34, Afghan 38, Sindh 43, Sikh 45, Burma 52, and "Peccavi".

Decode: 1775, 1776, 1782, 1802, 1803, 1817, 1818, 1848.

Chapter 14: Dalhousie (1848–56): Peak of Expansion (48)open chapter

1 · Peak of Expansion: how far and by what means

Memory hook

"Lapse, War, Lieu": the three roads of Paramountcy.

Decode: Lapse (refuse adopted heirs), Wars (Punjab 1848-49 and Pegu 1852-53), Lieu of subsidy (Berar 1853). Result: 63% of territory, 78% of population, and no more annexations after Dalhousie 48-56.

Why it sticks: Lapse and Lieu both start with L; the war sits between them.

Memory hook

63 and 78: "six-three of land, seven-eight of people".

Decode: 63% of India's territory, 78% of its population were under the Company State by 1856.

Why it sticks: the two numbers are the same digits reversed and moved (63, 78: 6+3 = 9, 7+8 = 15).

2 · The Doctrine of Lapse: legal principle against pretext

Memory hook

"Court Formulates 47, Dalhousie 48-56 Does 48".

Decode: Court of Directors formulates in 1847; Dalhousie 48-56 implements from 1848 (Satara is the first Lapse State, 1848). Rule: no biological heir = rights lapse; adoption under Hindu law is overridden.

Why it sticks: the court writes the law, the Governor General does the deed, one year apart (47, 48).

3 · Seven States in seven years (1848-54)

Memory hook

"Bhagat Sambhal, Ja Jhadio mein, Udta Sa Nag": the teacher's own line, with dates.

Decode: Baghat 1850, Sambalpur 1849, Jaitpur 1849, Jhansi 1854, Udaipur 1852, Satara 1848, Nagpur 1854. Count: 7 States in 7 years.

Why it sticks: say the line, then count on fingers: 50, 49, 49, 54, 52, 48, 54: the years only ever sit between 48 and 54.

Memory hook

Date ladder for Lapse: "48 Satara, 49 twins, 50 Baghat, 52 Udaipur, 54 twins".

Decode: 1848 Satara; 1849 Sambalpur and Jaitpur (twins); 1850 Baghat; 1852 Udaipur (Chhattisgarh); 1854 Jhansi and Nagpur (twins; Jhansi 1853-54).

Why it sticks: two twin years (49 and 54) with 50 and 52 in between; Satara opens, Jhansi and Nagpur close.

Memory hook

Prelims 2004 sequence: "Satara Sambalpur Jhansi = S-S-J = 3-2-1".

Decode: Satara 1848, Sambalpur 1849, Jhansi 1854; the question lists Jhansi (1), Sambalpur (2), Satara (3), so the true order reads 3, 2, 1: (c).

Why it sticks: the question's list is exactly backwards, so the answer is the countdown 3-2-1.

Memory hook

Udaipur is a "Chhattisgarh Udaipur", not Mewar.

Decode: Udaipur annexed 1852 by Lapse is in Chhattisgarh (then Central Provinces); the Rajput kingdom of Udaipur (Mewar) in Rajasthan was not annexed.

Why it sticks: the Lapse Udaipur is the "small, central" one; the famous one is in Rajasthan.

4 · The Inam Commission (1852)

Memory hook

Inam = "In-name": prove your name or lose it (1852, Bombay, 20,000).

Decode: 1852, Bombay Presidency, Inam Commission, tax-free grants to Brahmins and temples, proof of title demanded, about 20,000 estates seized.

Why it sticks: "Show your papers": no title papers, no estate; 20,000 is the headline number.

5 · The Doctrine of Lapse applied to pensions: Nana Sahib

Memory hook

"Eight lakh, fifty-one, Bithur": the Nana Sahib chain.

Decode: pension Rs 8 lakh a year; Bajirao II 96-18 died 1851; pensioned at Bithur in 1819; adopted son Nana Sahib refused, so he became an 1857 leader.

Why it sticks: 8 lakh, 51, 19, 57: four numbers around one town, Bithur.

6 · Annexation in lieu of subsidy: Berar (1853)

Memory hook

"98 signs, 53 pays in land": Hyderabad's subsidy line.

Decode: Hyderabad signs the first Subsidiary Alliance (1798), cannot pay by 1853, so Berar is taken as permanent payment. Same trick as Awadh 1801.

Why it sticks: 98 to 53 is a 55-year slide from signature to seizure.

7 · The annexation of Awadh (1856)

Memory hook

Awadh A, B, then "N-U-U-U": the naming ladder.

Decode: A = 1801 Ceded Districts (NWP 1836, Agra), B = 1856 rest (Lucknow); 1877 NWP and Oudh; 1902 United Provinces of Agra and Oudh; 1937 United Provinces; 1950 Uttar Pradesh; 2000 Uttarakhand carved out.

Why it sticks: after 1877 the dates run 02, 37, 50, 00: like a phone number; the name shrinks each time (Agra and Oudh, then just United).

Memory hook

Awadh's six steps: "Clive 57-67 Starts, Shore Swaps, Wellesley 98-05 Wipes, Dalhousie 48-56 Dumps".

Decode: Clive 57-67 (1765) starts mini SA + PoIR + PoRF; Shore 93-98 (1798) swaps Wazir Ali 97-98 for Sadat Ali Khan II (Rs 76 lakh, Allahabad fort); Wellesley 98-05 (1801) takes half (Ceded Districts: Gorakhpur, Rohilkhand, Doab); Dalhousie 48-56 (1856) takes the rest on misgovernance. Revenue was twice the subsidy.

Why it sticks: four officials, four verbs, the S-S-W-D ladder.

Memory hook

76 lakh, then double.

Decode: Rs 76 lakh annual subsidy (1798) and Allahabad fort ceded; in 1801 the Ceded Districts' revenue was twice the subsidy: unjust.

Why it sticks: first the number (76), then the doubling that makes it unfair.

Memory hook

Heirs mean "blame", no heirs mean "lapse".

Decode: Awadh 1856 = maladministration pretext, because Wajid Ali Shah had heirs and Lapse could not apply (Prelims 2008: Awadh). With the Resident's Indirect Rule since 1855, he had no power but all responsibility.

Why it sticks: a ruler with a son cannot lapse, so he is blamed instead.

8.1 Buffer to target

Memory hook

"Four factions fell the Sikh State": R-S-D-K.

Decode: Royal family (succession fights), Sindhanwaliyas (a Jat clan or misl), Dogra Rajputs (Wazirs), Khalsa Army. After Ranjit Singh 01-39's death in 1839.

Why it sticks: four initials, R-S-D-K, one per faction.

Memory hook

Indirect rule list "N-A-P" (1814-16, 1839, 1845) against annexation list "A-P-B" (1824-26, 1849, 1852-53).

Decode: Indirect Rule = Nepal 1814-16, Afghanistan 1839, Punjab 1845. Direct annexation = Assam and parts of lower Burma 1824-26, Punjab 1849, Burma 1852-53. Punjab appears on both lists: first Indirect Rule, then annexed.

Why it sticks: Punjab is the only name on both lists: first Indirect Rule, then annexed.

8.2 The First Anglo-Sikh War and the Treaty of Lahore (9 March 1846)

Memory hook

Lahore 9, Amritsar 16: "nine-sixteen" in March 1846.

Decode: 9 March 1846 Treaty of Lahore (army cut, Jalandhar Doab, Kashmir ceded, Resident Henry Lawrence). 16 March 1846 Treaty of Amritsar (Kashmir sold to Gulab Singh for 75 lakh Nanakshahee rupees). Not to be confused with Amritsar 1809 (Sutlej boundary).

Why it sticks: Lahore is first (9), Amritsar one week later (16); 1809 is the old one.

Memory hook

"MJKR": the four clauses of the Treaty of Lahore.

Decode: Military restraint (Khalsa reduced, EIC army stationed), Jalandhar Doab annexed, Kashmir mechanism (indemnity Rs 1.5 crore unpaid, Kashmir and Hazara ceded then sold for 75 lakh to Gulab Singh), Resident (Henry Lawrence, PoIR in Punjab).

Why it sticks: M-J-K-R follows the clause order 1 to 4.

Memory hook

Indemnity 1.5 crore, sale 75 lakh: "half price".

Decode: war indemnity Rs 1.5 crore; Kashmir and Hazara sold to Gulab Singh for 75 lakh Nanakshahee rupees, exactly half.

Why it sticks: Kashmir is sold at half the bill.

8.3 The Second Anglo-Sikh War (1848-49)

Memory hook

"Mulraj and Chattar rebel: Dalhousie 48-56 smiles."

Decode: Mulraj (Multan) and Chattar Singh (Haripur) revolted; result 1849: Punjab annexed from Duleep Singh 43-49 (child of 5), Koh-i-Noor to the Crown, Board of Administration (the Lawrence Brothers and Charles Mansel). The Khyber Pass frontier is now British.

Why it sticks: "M and C" are the two rebels; "D" (Dalhousie 48-56) delights.

Memory hook

Duleep 43-49: five years old, six years on the throne.

Decode: Maharaja Duleep Singh 43-49 was a child of 5 at accession; his code runs 43 to 49, and 1849 is the year Punjab was annexed and the Koh-i-Noor went to the British Crown.

Why it sticks: the code's end year (49) is the annexation year, so his reign and his State end together.

9 · The Second Anglo-Burmese War (1852-53): Lower Burma and Pegu

Memory hook

Burma in three wars: "Arakan-Tenasserim 24, Pegu 52, Upper Burma Dufferin 84-88".

Decode: 1st war 1824-26 (Arakan, Tenasserim, Treaty of Yandabo 1826); 2nd war 1852-53 (Pegu, Dalhousie 48-56); 3rd war (Upper Burma, Dufferin 84-88). Burma separated 1937 (GOI Act 1935).

Why it sticks: 24, 52, and "Duff" as the last: three steps up the river, south to north.

Memory hook

Bay of Bengal sweep "B-N-C-O-B-P": Bengal, Northern Sarkars, Carnatic, Orissa, Burma, Pegu.

Decode: Bengal 1757, Northern Sarkars 1766, Carnatic 1801, Orissa 1803, Burma (Arakan, Tenasserim) 1824-26, Pegu 1852-53. Hyderabad's 1798 SA sits beside the coast.

Why it sticks: clockwise from Calcutta down and round to Burma: "Big Names Come Over, Boats Pass".

10.1 The Indian Ocean coastline (Bay of Bengal and Arabian Sea, 1668-1853)

Memory hook

Two sweeps and one beyond: "Bay clockwise from Bengal, Sea anticlockwise from Bombay".

Decode: Bay of Bengal clockwise: Bengal 1757, Northern Sarkars 1766, Carnatic 1801, Orissa 1803-05, Burma 1824-26 and 1852-53. Arabian Sea anticlockwise: Bombay 1668, Mysore/Malabar 1789-99, Gaikwad-Baroda and Peshwa SA 1802, Travancore 1805, Svarajya 1818, Sindh 1843. Beyond India: Kalat 1854, Jacobabad 1876, Persia 1856-57, 1907, 1909.

Why it sticks: the two coasts meet at the tip, as if a hand closes around the peninsula; the third panel is what lies outside the fist.

10.2 Sea approaches beyond India: Balochistan and the Persian Gulf (self read)

Memory hook

Kalat 54 pays, Jacobabad 76 rules: "Pay then Pack".

Decode: Treaty of Kalat 1854 (EIC, Major John Jacob, Dalhousie 48-56): the EIC pays the Khan Rs 50,000 a year; he gives up foreign policy and allows outposts. Treaty of Jacobabad 1876 (British State, Robert Sandeman, Lytton 76-80): troops, railways, telegraphs, arbitration over the Sardars: the Resident is the court.

Why it sticks: 1854 is the EIC era (company pays), 1876 is the Crown era (crown commands); 22 years apart.

Memory hook

Gwadar: gifted 83, sold 58, held by Oman between.

Decode: 1783 Khan of Kalat gifts Gwadar to a prince of Oman; 1958 Pakistan buys it from Oman, 11 years after Independence; over 170 years an Omani enclave, never owned by the British.

Why it sticks: 83 and 58 are the same digits flipped, like Company 65, Crown 58 in Chapter 10.

Memory hook

Persia in three steps: "Force 56-57, Split 07, Oil 09".

Decode: 1856-57 Anglo-Persian War (Bushehr, Kharg Island), Treaty of Paris 1857 (Herat given up); 1907 Anglo-Russian Convention (southern coast in the British sphere); 1909 Anglo-Persian Oil Company (BP).

Why it sticks: force, then paper, then money: each cheaper than the last.

The Mains 2013 skeleton

Memory hook

"RTPP-WU": Dalhousie 48-56's moderniser list.

Decode: Railways (Bombay-Thane 1853), Telegraph (1853, 4,000 miles), Post Office Act (1854, half-anna), PWD (civilian; GT Road, canals), Wood's Despatch (1854), Universities (groundwork; set up 1857 under Canning 56-62).

Why it sticks: "R-T-P-P-W-U" as "Rail, Tar, Post, Pay, Wood, Uni" in the order of the slide.

Memory hook

Three rails: "West 53, East 56, South 56".

Decode: West: Bombay-Thane (1853). East: Calcutta-Raniganj (by 1856, coal-rich). South: Madras-Arakkonam (by 1856). The teacher drew exactly this West, East, South board.

Why it sticks: the first is the odd one out in time: 53 first, then both others in 56.

Memory hook

Half-anna, two paise, Peshawar to Calcutta: the GT Road's twin.

Decode: Post Office Act 1854: a uniform half-anna (2 paise) rate over the same line as the GT Road (Peshawar to Calcutta), which the PWD (now civilian) restored.

Why it sticks: the letter and the road run the same line, west to east.

Memory hook

Girls' school ladder: Phule 1848, Bethune 1849, Vidyasagar 1851, 1856.

Decode: 1 Jan 1848 Savitribai and Jyotirao Phule's Pune school (first girls' school overall); 1849 Bethune School, Calcutta (J.E. Drinkwater Bethune, Law Member): first successful secular school for girls; 1851 Vidyasagar Principal of Sanskrit College; 1856 Inspector of Schools and the Hindu Widow Remarriage Act.

Why it sticks: four years in ascending order from 48 to 56, each one step later.

12.1 Relative Isolation (until 1813)

Memory hook

Isolation "rules of thumb": Trade, Temporary, Treaty.

Decode: Trade is the primary motive; interference is Temporary (when an opportunity arises, then revert); the tools are Treaties (Allahabad 1765, Surat 1775, Purandhar 1776, Salbai 1782). Proofs: small army (Child's War 1686-90); 1st Anglo-Maratha War 1775-82 and Buxar 1764 as the "opportunities".

Why it sticks: three T's, one per hand-written circled point.

Memory hook

Treaty dates of the isolation era in a row: 65, 75, 76, 82.

Decode: Allahabad 1765 (alliance), Surat 1775 (succession dispute), Purandhar 1776 (Hastings 73-85 settle commercially), Salbai 1782 (alliance; also PoRF with the Marathas).

Why it sticks: 65, 75, 76, 82: two twenty-fives then six, then seven more.

12.2 Policy of Indirect Rule (PoIR) and Policy of Ring Fence (PoRF)

Memory hook

PoIR places in order: "Ben-Del-Tra-Sat-Pun-Awa": 72, 03, 09, 18, 46, 55.

Decode: Bengal by 1772, Delhi 1803, Travancore by 1809, Satara by 1818, Punjab 1846, Awadh by 1855. A British Resident in court; no formal annexation.

Why it sticks: the years climb 72, 03, 09, 18, 46, 55 with gaps of 31, 6, 9, 28, 9; Satara and Punjab and Awadh all end up annexed later.

Memory hook

PoRF buffer chain: "Awadh 65, Hyderabad 66, Mysore 69, Marathas 82, Sikhs 1809".

Decode: Awadh (ToA 1765), Hyderabad (Treaty of Hyderabad 1766), Mysore (Treaty of Madras 1769), Marathas (Treaty of Salbai 1782), Sikh (Treaty of Amritsar 1809). Each is a buffer around British India against an outside threat.

Why it sticks: "65, 66, 69, 82, 09" spoken like jersey numbers walking around the border clockwise.

12.3 Policy of Subsidiary Alliance (PoSA, 1798-1858)

Memory hook

SA: "Promise Non-interference, Practice Interference".

Decode: PoSA takes foreign policy and military, paid by subsidy, promises non-interference; broken by illegal PoIR. Bajirao II 96-18: Bassein 1802 (to regain his throne), Elphinstone bypasses him, 1817 the 3rd Anglo-Maratha War. "No powers, all blame" = Awadh 1856.

Why it sticks: the same two words twice (non-interference then interference): the promise and the break.

12.4 Policy of Paramountcy / Subordinate Isolation (1813-58)

Memory hook

"PoP = SA minus rights"; Francis Hastings 13-23 starts it in 1813.

Decode: Paramountcy = Subsidiary Alliance minus the Indian State's rights; sealed bubble: no talk, treaty or alliance without the EIC as gatekeeper; started by Francis Hastings 13-23 in 1813; the teacher: it contributed to the 3rd Anglo-Maratha War 1817-19.

Why it sticks: subtraction sign: SA is the full price, take away the rights and you get PoP.

12.5 Policy of Subordinate Union (1858-1947)

Memory hook

Prince = son of the King, and the King is in London.

Decode: from 1858 Indian "Kings" become Indian Princes because the British Crown is the monarch of Indian kings (not the Mughal). 565 princely States are ruled by Indian Princes; British India is ruled by the British. External functions D-C-FA go to the Crown.

Why it sticks: "Prince" literally means junior to a King; the King is the Crown.

Memory hook

"D-C-FA": what Subordinate Union takes (Defence, Communications, Foreign Affairs).

Decode: Defence, Communications, Foreign Affairs = external functions with the British Crown; domestic sovereign functions stay with the Indian Prince. Subordinate Union = Paramountcy + sovereignty with the Crown.

Why it sticks: "D-C-FA" reads like a three-letter department: "DC and FA".

12.6 The arc in five steps

Memory hook

"Alone, Ally, Army, All-ruler, Absorber": the five steps.

Decode: 1600 Alone (left alone, isolation); 1765 Ally (military alliance partner); 1798 Army (controls neighbours' armies and foreign policy); 1813 All-ruler (Paramountcy: supreme); 1858 Absorber (the Crown absorbs 565 princes).

Why it sticks: five A-words, one per step: Alone, Ally, Army, All, Absorb.

Memory hook

1600, 1765, 1798, 1813, 1858: "Sixteen hundred, then 65, 98, 13, 58".

Decode: the five dates of the policy arc: 1600, 1765, 1798, 1813, 1858. Gaps: 165, 33, 15, 45.

Why it sticks: listen to the rhythm: the middle two (98 and 13) are the quickest steps, only 15 years apart.

13.1 Consolidation at the cost of legitimacy

Memory hook

"Lakshmibai, Nana, Zafar": the three annexation-aggrieved names (Lapse 1854, Pension 1851, Dignity).

Decode: Lakshmibai (Jhansi Lapse 1854), Nana Sahib (Bajirao II 96-18's pension of Rs 8 lakh refused 1851), Bahadur Shah Zafar 37-57 (the last Mughal's titular dignity). Plus the Awadh court, disbanded Sikh soldiers, talukdars and inam-holders.

Why it sticks: the three grievances are Throne, Pension, Title: L-N-Z = Land, Name, Zafar.

13.2 Post-Dalhousie: Canning 56-62 inherits and the Queen ends Lapse

Memory hook

58 is the reversal: Act, Proclamation, Union.

Decode: 1858: the Government of India Act abolishes the Company State; Queen Victoria's Proclamation ends Lapse and respects treaties; Paramountcy gives way to Subordinate Union. After Dalhousie 48-56 (Canning 56-62 inherits) there are no further annexations.

Why it sticks: three things in one year, each undoing what Dalhousie 48-56 built.

14.1 Prelims 1996: Governor General and Viceroy match

Memory hook

GG ladder by title: Fort William, India, Viceroy, Crown Rep.

Decode: 1773 Regulating Act = GG of Fort William (Cornwallis 86-93); 1833 Charter Act = GG of India (Dalhousie 48-56); 1858 = GG and Viceroy (Minto 05-10); 1935 = GG and Crown Representative (Wavell 43-47). Order of codes: 86, 48, 05, 43.

Why it sticks: four titles, four people, with Dalhousie 48-56 in the second slot: "Fort, India, Viceroy, Crown".

14.2 Prelims 2002: person and event match

Memory hook

Prelims 2002: "Mac 32, Lin 40, Dal Lapse, Chelm 19".

Decode: Macdonald = Communal Award 1932; Linlithgow 36-43 = August Offer 1940; Dalhousie 48-56 = Doctrine of Lapse; Chelmsford 16-21 = Dyarchy 1919. Code A-2 B-3 C-1 D-4.

Why it sticks: years climb 19, 32, 40 as the codes run 4, 2, 3.

14.3 Prelims 2004: "NOT correctly matched"

Memory hook

Vernacular Press 78, Lytton 76-80: "78 sits inside 76-80".

Decode: the Vernacular Press Act 1878 falls inside Lytton 76-80's years (76 to 80), so it cannot be Curzon 99-05's. The other three pairs: Pitt's Act 1784 (Warren Hastings 73-85), Lapse (Dalhousie 48-56), Ilbert Bill (Ripon 80-84).

Why it sticks: put 78 between 76 and 80 and the F is obvious.

14.5 Prelims 1995: battle pairs

Memory hook

Prelims 1995: "Wandiwash 60 French; Buxar 64 not Jafar; Chilianwala 49 Sikh; Kharda 95 not EIC".

Decode: Wandiwash 1760: French vs EIC (the only true pair). Buxar 1764: not "Mir Jafar 57-60 vs Clive 57-67". Chilianwala 1849: 2nd Anglo-Sikh War, not Marathas. Kharda 1795: not Nizam vs EIC.

Why it sticks: each wrong pair gets a one-word reason: "not Jafar", "Sikh", "not EIC".

Memory hooks: chapter in one breath

Memory hook

The story of "Dalhousie 48-56's three roads, two coasts, one crack": from Paramountcy to 1857.

A trading company wanted to be left Alone (1600), became an Ally (1765), then an Army that ran its neighbours (SA 1798), then the All-ruler (Paramountcy 1813, Francis Hastings 13-23): by 1856 it held 63% of land and 78% of people.

Then Dalhousie 48-56 went down his three roads: Lapse (Court of Directors 1847: Satara 48, 49 twins, Baghat 50, Udaipur 52, Jhansi and Nagpur 54), War (Lahore 9 March 1846 and Amritsar 16 March, then Punjab 1849 and the Koh-i-Noor; Pegu 1852-53), and Lieu (Berar 1853).

Awadh fell by misgovernance, not Lapse: Clive 57-67's 1765, Shore's Rs 76 lakh 1798, Wellesley 98-05's half in 1801, then the rest in 1856.

Round the coast the maritime fence closed (Bay clockwise, Sea anticlockwise, Kalat 1854 then Jacobabad 1876), and Dalhousie 48-56 laid rail, wire, post and Wood's Despatch.

But Lakshmibai, Nana Sahib and Zafar carried the grievance into 1857, and in 1858 the Crown reversed Lapse and became the Absorber of 565 princes.

Decode: 1847, 1848-54, 1856, 1857, 1858.

Chapter 15: British Imperial Ideology (44)open chapter

0 · The chapter on one board

Memory hook

"Orient, Authority, Liberal-Utility-Evangel, Racist": O-A-L-R.

Decode: Orientalism (1765) then Authoritarianism (Cornwallis 86-93, 1786-93) then the Liberal-Utilitarian-Evangelical trio (from 1813) then Racism plus Authoritarianism (after 1857-58).

Why it sticks: OALR rhymes with "oar", and the empire rows on under the White Man's Burden roof.

2.1 The roots of British civilisational confidence

Memory hook

"Democracy, Machines, War, Napoleon, Science": D-M-W-N-S.

Decode: first modern Democracy (Civil War + Glorious Revolution, 17th century); Machines = Industrial Revolution (c. 1750-1850); Won the Seven Years' Global War (1756-63); Napoleon beaten (Leipzig 1813, Waterloo 1815); Science and capitalism.

Why it sticks: five proud steps in time order, the 17th century to 1815.

Memory hook

Leipzig 13, Waterloo 15.

Decode: Britain financed the coalition that won at Leipzig (1813) and led the allied armies with Prussia to final victory at Waterloo (1815).

Why it sticks: the two years are two steps apart, 13 then 15: money first, sword second.

2.2 WMB as roof, and the counter-ideologies as reaction

Memory hook

"A-F-E-I": the four counter-currents.

Decode: American Revolution (from 1765; war 1776-83), French Revolution (1789), European revolutions (1830 and 1848), Irish Home Rule (1870-1914; armed struggle 1916-21).

Why it sticks: "AFEI" is simply the order in time.

3.1 Orientalism defined

Memory hook

"HILL": the four things Orientalism ruled by.

Decode: Hindu law, Islamic law, Indian Languages, Indian syllabus (Learning in schools and colleges).

Why it sticks: the teacher's four bullets fit the word HILL; Orientalism = "go up the hill of Indian tradition".

3.2 Why the EIC adopted Orientalism

Memory hook

"Scared and Spared": two reasons for Orientalism.

Decode: the EIC was scared (not militarily confident before 1813; one insult could spark mass resistance) and wanted to spare India from alien systems (some Englishmen thought India not ready for British modernity). Champion: Warren Hastings 73-85, "lighter chains".

Why it sticks: scared for self, sparing the Indian.

3.2.1 The four Orientalist institutions

Memory hook

"1781-84-91-1800: Mad, Asia, Sanskrit, Fort."

Decode: 1781 Calcutta Madrasa (Warren Hastings 73-85); 1784 Asiatic Society (William Jones); 1791 Sanskrit College Banaras (Jonathan Duncan); 1800 Fort William College (Wellesley 98-05).

Why it sticks: the years climb 81, 84, 91 and then jump to the century mark, 1800.

Memory hook

Who wore which hat.

Decode: Hastings 73-85 = Governor-General; Jones = Puisne Judge; Duncan = Resident at Benares; Wellesley 98-05 = Governor-General. Two GGs, one judge, one Resident.

Why it sticks: the "capacity" column is a favourite trap, and two of the four are GGs.

3.3 Orientalism's political angle

Memory hook

"Cousins come home."

Decode: Aryan Invasion Theory = Europeans brought India's greatness; kinship through language = "we are cousins who have come home". Real purpose: a rhetoric of love to disguise exploitation.

Why it sticks: an exploiter who calls himself family.

Memory hook

Fort William = Fitness for Work: train civilians.

Decode: Fort William College (1800, Wellesley 98-05) trains newly recruited EIC civil servants in Indian languages and administration. Not Board orders, not oriental revival, not Carey's job.

Why it sticks: a college for new recruits = a training camp, not a museum.

4.1 The Cornwallis 86-93 shift

Memory hook

"A-R-P-A": Cornwallis 86-93's four steps.

Decode: All-British senior posts (Indians removed); Rule of law by codification (Charter Act 1793; Collector is no longer Judge); Permanent Settlement 1793 (zamindars as English-style landlords); Aloof Elite (no mixing, no Indian wives, Eurasians barred, no land purchase for officers with Indian wives).

Why it sticks: "ARPA" like "Arpa", one tidy list of four.

Memory hook

Collector or Judge, not both.

Decode: Charter Act 1793 (Cornwallis 86-93's influence) separated powers at the district level: the District Collector no longer also functions as the District Judge.

Why it sticks: one chair, one job.

4.2 The Aloof-Elite doctrine

Memory hook

"Lost America, so no roots in India."

Decode: Cornwallis 86-93 lost in the American War of Independence (1776-83); British settlers in the 13 colonies grew roots and fought for their homeland; so in India: no mixing, no marriage, no land, never let India become home.

Why it sticks: a lost war turned into a rule.

4.3 Conservatism: Munro, Malcolm, Elphinstone, Metcalfe

Memory hook

"Indian coat, British hand."

Decode: Munro's Conservatism = Orientalist in form (Indian traditions, Ryotwari's direct State-peasant model) and Authoritarian in substance (all collection by EIC officials; no Indians in senior roles). School: Munro, Malcolm, Elphinstone, Metcalfe (Elphinstone took Ryotwari to Bombay).

Why it sticks: the coat is Indian, the hand inside it is British.

5.1 The post-1813 sharp turn

Memory hook

"Four wins, one market": why 1813 turned.

Decode: four changes (Indian powers beaten except Holkar, Bhonsle, Sikh State; a huge army; Napoleon beaten; Industrial Revolution in full swing) plus one motive: a market for British goods through western tastes and education.

Why it sticks: strength first, then shopkeeping.

Memory hook

Three left standing: H-B-S.

Decode: Holkar, Bhonsle, Sikh State were the only potential rivals left by 1813.

Why it sticks: "HBS" like a station code, three stops left.

5.2 Liberalism

Memory hook

"Law, Learn, Lift the monopoly": Macaulay's three.

Decode: Law Commission (1834; codified western-style laws by 1837); Learn = Minute on Education (1835; English; Brown Sahibs); Lift the monopoly = Charter Act 1813 (non-EIC British businesses allowed in).

Why it sticks: three L-words in a row.

Memory hook

Brown skin, British mind: "Brown Sahib".

Decode: Macaulay's Minute on Education 1835: western education in English to create Indians who are brown outside and British inside, cultural go-betweens for the rulers and the masses.

Why it sticks: the phrase itself is the picture.

Memory hook

Three feudals: P-E-S.

Decode: Liberals wanted to end feudal Polity (no democracy), Economy (agrarian, closed, unequal) and Society (birth-based discrimination, no women's rights).

Why it sticks: "PES" = polity, economy, society, the old triple.

Memory hook

1770 and "doubled".

Decode: Liberals' proof that Orientalism governed badly: the Great Bengal Famine 1770, one crore perished while the EIC doubled land revenue.

Why it sticks: crore dead, revenue doubled: the two numbers sit in one line.

5.3 Utilitarianism

Memory hook

"Mill, the Schoolmaster": History 1818.

Decode: James Mill's History of British India (1818) called India's great past a myth and said India needed an effective schoolmaster imposing good governance from above; as a Director in London he pushed the Law Commission 1834 under Macaulay.

Why it sticks: one teacher, one cane, one book in 1818.

5.4 Evangelicalism

Memory hook

Charter 1813: Grant opens the gate; Lex Loci 1850 clears the road.

Decode: Charles Grant (Director, later MP) influenced the Charter Act 1813 to legalise missionary entry; the Lex Loci Act 1850 let a convert keep inheritance rights in family property.

Why it sticks: gate first (1813), then the road (1850).

Memory hook

Three ideas, three verbs after 1813: L-U-E.

Decode: Liberalism = give the best (= west) ideas; Utilitarianism = good laws and a strong State; Evangelicalism = Christianize to modernize.

Why it sticks: "LUE" = clue; the clue is one verb each.

6 · The Liberalism shift 1828-35

Memory hook

"Sati, Thugs, English, Code": the 1828-35 four.

Decode: Sati Abolition 1829; Thugs suppressed (Sleeman); English replaces Persian in higher instruction (Minute 1835); Code: Cornwallis 86-93 Code revised into uniform criminal procedure (base for the 1834 Law Commission).

Why it sticks: S-T-E-C, in the order of the handout list.

7.1 The six-front sharp shift after 1857

Memory hook

"R-R-A-D-A-S": the six fronts.

Decode: Reform retreated; Racial sciences (language to physical features); Aryan theory twisted; Dogs and Indians not allowed; Authoritarianism up; Social reforms slowed (Queen's Proclamation 1858).

Why it sticks: the six initials follow the handout order exactly.

Memory hook

1858: the Queen promises not to meddle.

Decode: Queen Victoria's Proclamation of 1858: religious tolerance and non-interference in Indian customs, because missionary and reformer interference was a major grievance of 1857.

Why it sticks: after the fire, the promise of "hands off".

7.2 Post-1857 architects: Lytton 76-80 and Curzon 99-05

Memory hook

"L78, C05": two Viceroys, two marks.

Decode: Lytton 76-80 = Vernacular Press Act and Arms Act, both 1878; Curzon 99-05 = Partition of Bengal 1905. Both = prestige, paramountcy, racial hierarchy.

Why it sticks: the initials and the last two digits: L-78, C-05.

8 · Law and order paternalism: Sleeman and the Thugs

Memory hook

"I-A-S" for Sleeman: Informant, Approver, Swift commission.

Decode: Informant networks; Approver testimony (lighter sentence for evidence); Swift judicial commissions. Department set up 1835; over 3,000 convicted 1831-41.

Why it sticks: Sleeman's tools spell IAS.

Memory hook

Sleeman = Thugs, Prendergast = Burma, Burnes = Afghanistan.

Decode: S-T, P-B, B-A: the three names and their three places. Handout hook, copied as written.

Why it sticks: say it as one line, S-P-B, and the places follow: Thugs, Burma, Afghanistan.

9.1 The Treaty of Gandamak 1879

Memory hook

Gandamak 1879: "R-A-F-K-6".

Decode: Resident stationed; Army stationed; Foreign policy taken; Khyber Pass (and some other lands) to the British; 6 lakh a year paid to the Amir. A mini subsidiary alliance, 1879, in the 2nd war (1878-80).

Why it sticks: "RAF-K-6": the RAF flies over the Khyber, the cheque is six lakh.

Memory hook

1839-42 and 1878-80: "39 and 78 are 39 years apart."

Decode: 1st Anglo-Afghan War 1839-42 (Auckland 36-42: Shah Shuja against Dost Mohammad; January 1842 retreat from Kabul) and the 2nd Anglo-Afghan War 1878-80 (Lytton 76-80; Gandamak 1879; Cavagnari killed).

Why it sticks: 1839 plus 39 = 1878, close enough to place both wars.

Memory hook

Lytton 76-80 = Forward; Ripon 80-84 = Retreat.

Decode: Lytton 76-80 carried forward the Forward Policy (2nd Anglo-Afghan War); Ripon 80-84 reversed it and withdrew.

Why it sticks: handout hook, copied as written; the two terms are consecutive (76-80, 80-84).

9.2 Revision: Russophobia and the Great Game

Memory hook

Offence and defence: Forward vs Ring Fence.

Decode: Russophobia / Great Game. Offence = Forward Policy (push the frontier out, two Afghan wars 1839-42 and 1878-80). Defence = Ring Fence (friendly buffer States: Afghanistan and Punjab; the 1838 Tripartite Treaty). Persia: Anglo-Persian War 1856-57 and the 1907 Convention.

Why it sticks: one team, a striker and a goalkeeper.

Memory hook

Iran cut in three, 1907: "Russia up, Neutral mid, British down".

Decode: Anglo-Russian Convention 1907: north = Russian sphere; middle = neutral buffer; south = British sphere.

Why it sticks: top to bottom: R, N, B.

10 · Dufferin 84-88 and Upper Burma (1886)

Memory hook

Dufferin 84-88 = 1886 = Upper Burma = the last.

Decode: Dufferin 84-88 added Upper Burma in January 1886 after the 3rd Anglo-Burmese War 1885-86; Thibaw to Ratnagiri; Prendergast commanded; merged with Lower Burma (British from 1852) until the 1937 separation.

Why it sticks: handout hook, copied as written; "Dufferin 84-88 ends, Dalhousie 48-56 began" for the order.

Memory hook

Burma in three dates: 1852, 1886, 1937.

Decode: 1852 Lower Burma British; 1886 Upper Burma annexed (Dufferin 84-88); 1937 Burma separated from British India.

Why it sticks: first half, second half, then the split.

11 · Imperial Preference

Memory hook

Imperial Preference = tariff = British imports first.

Decode: Britain, Dominions and India gave each other lower tariffs than non-Empire rivals; formalised at Ottawa 1932; British goods paid less than German, Japanese or American goods at Indian ports.

Why it sticks: handout hook, copied as written; "Ottawa 32" = a trade fence in the year 1932.

11.1 The stray table of five antiquarians (Prelims 2023)

Memory hook

"Rea, Long, Sew, Bur, El": Rea digs, Long digs, Sewell writes, Burgess heads, Elliot catalogues.

Decode: Rea = Amaravati and Bhattiprolu; Longhurst = Nagarjunakonda and Hampi; Sewell = A Forgotten Empire (Vijayanagara); Burgess = first Director of the Archaeological Survey of Western and Southern India; Elliot = Madras Civil Service, Andhra Buddhist sites and coins.

Why it sticks: Rea and Longhurst are the two diggers; the rest are writer, head, collector. All five = archaeology (answer a).

12.3 Returning Indian leaders brought these currents home

Memory hook

"P-E-S then People": the Mains 2014 answer frame.

Decode: Political (American, French, 1830 and 1848, Irish); Economic (Industrial Revolution's handloom destruction, Drain Theory of Naoroji and Dutt); Social and ideological (Russia 1917, Wilson 1918, anti-racism); then People (Gandhi from South Africa, Nehru from Cambridge).

Why it sticks: the three buckets are the same P-E-S as the feudal triple in Section 5.2, now turned against the British.

Memory hook

Gandhi 93-15, Nehru Cambridge.

Decode: Gandhi's South African struggles ran 1893-1915; Nehru absorbed western liberal-democratic theory at Cambridge.

Why it sticks: 93 to 15 is the 22-year apprenticeship, the same range as in Chapter 35.

13 · Summary: the eight imperial ideologies

Memory hook

"WOACLUER": eight ideologies in order.

Decode: WMB (foundation), Orientalism, Authoritarianism, Conservatism, Liberalism, Utilitarianism, Evangelicalism, Racism. Years: 1765, 1786, 1801, then 1813 three times, then post 1857.

Why it sticks: read it as "wow, a clue, er": the order is the handout's own.

Memory hook

Three 1813s.

Decode: Liberalism, Utilitarianism and Evangelicalism all begin 1813 and all run to 1857; the Charter Act 1813 is the date every one of them leans on.

Why it sticks: one year, three ideologies, one Act.

Memory hook

Champion pairs: H, C, M, M, G, L.

Decode: Hastings 73-85 = Orientalism; Cornwallis 86-93 = Authoritarianism; Munro = Conservatism; Macaulay = Liberalism (with Bentinck 28-35); Bentham and James Mill = Utilitarianism; Grant = Evangelicalism; Lytton 76-80 and Curzon 99-05 = post-1857.

Why it sticks: initials in time order down the summary table.

Memory hooks: chapter in one breath

Memory hook

The story of "The Burden that Changed its Coat".

The British pick up the White Man's Burden (Britain first in the Industrial Revolution 1750-1850, won 1756-63, beat Napoleon at Leipzig 1813 and Waterloo 1815) and ask "how should we civilise India?"

At first they are scared: Orientalism (1765-1813), Warren Hastings 73-85 and Jones: Madrasa 1781, Asiatic Society 1784, Sanskrit College 1791, Fort William 1800. Rule India the India Way.

Cornwallis 86-93 says My way or highway: no Indians in senior posts, Collector is not Judge, Permanent Settlement 1793, an Aloof Elite, because he lost America.

After 1813 the EIC is confident: Liberalism (Macaulay: Law 1834, Minute 1835), Utilitarianism (Mill's schoolmaster 1818) and Evangelicalism (Grant 1813, Lex Loci 1850), and the Sleeman police net chases the Thugs.

Then 1857 shocks them: "Dogs and Indians not allowed", the Queen's 1858 promise, Lytton 76-80 and Curzon 99-05; the Forward Policy runs from Auckland 36-42 to Ripon 80-84's retreat; Dufferin 84-88 ends the land-grab at Upper Burma 1886; Ottawa 1932 locks in Imperial Preference. And the world's revolutions come home with Gandhi and Nehru.

Decode: 1765, 1781-1800, 1793, 1813, 1829-35, 1857-58, 1878, 1886, 1905, 1932.

Chapter 16: Land Revenue Systems (1765–1885) (52)open chapter

0 · The chapter on one board

Memory hook

Lazy Aunts Sip Chai Together: the five questions.

Decode: Landowner, Assessment, Settlement (with whom), Collection (by whom), Tenants' rights.

Why it sticks: the same five questions fit every system in the chapter, so the sentence is your answer-skeleton.

1 · The Mughal blueprint for land revenue

Memory hook

K-P-I-J: the chain climbs "Kisan, Primary, Intermediary, Jagirdar".

Decode: Khudkasht pays to Primary Zamindar (plus abwabs), who keeps a commission and passes up to the Intermediary Zamindar, who keeps a cut and passes up to the Jagirdar (jagir land) or the State (khalisa land). Amils and Amalguzars check that nobody under-reports.

Why it sticks: every step takes a bite except the last, which is the receiver.

Memory hook

Mughal = Survey, Gross, Revisable; Ijaradari = Auction.

Decode: Mughal assessment = detailed survey, % of gross produce (one-third), revisable; collected by Zamindars. Ijaradari = summary assessment by auction, settlement and collection with the Ijaradar. The British used the second for 1772.

Why it sticks: survey versus auction, careful versus hasty.

2.1 Experiment 1: Dual Government (1765-72)

Memory hook

Greed, Drought, Double: why 1770 happened.

Decode: Greed (patta ignored, rents beyond the record) + Drought (back-to-back) + Double (revenue demand doubled in 1770, with brutality) = Great Bengal Famine 1770, 50 lakh to 1 crore. Reza Khan jailed 1772-74 as the scapegoat.

Why it sticks: three Ds, one death toll.

Memory hook

N-F-E, then 1.4 at 4: the bankruptcy of 1772.

Decode: Nabobs' corruption + Famine of 1770 + Expenses (army and Dual System) = bankrupt by 1772. Bailout: 1.4 million pounds at 4%, condition: parliamentary oversight, so the Regulating Act 1773.

Why it sticks: "one-four at four" repeats the 4; the condition is the lock on the loan.

2.2 Experiment 2: the Farming System of Warren Hastings 73-85 (1772)

Memory hook

Bid, Bleed, Bust: the Farming System.

Decode: Revenue Farmers bid at auction (assessment = auction, settlement = Revenue Farmer); they bled the ryots to recover the bid and profit; demand so high peasants paid nothing, so the system went bust. Supervisor: a European DC.

Why it sticks: three verbs, same order as the outcome.

2.3 Primer: tax, property, settlement (taught first in the lecture)

Memory hook

Rent climbs one step, Tax climbs the next.

Decode: Tenant pays rent to the property owner; the owner pays tax to the State. Rent is borne by the tenant; tax is always borne by the owner. A salary is only for employees.

Why it sticks: a two-rung ladder: Tenant, Owner, State.

Memory hook

Zamindar's three lives: Collector, Owner, Abolished.

Decode: before 1793 collector (10% commission), 1793+ legal landowner (conditional), 1947+ the big-landownership class abolished.

Why it sticks: C, O, A in order, with 1793 as the hinge.

Memory hook

SAP: three assessment forks.

Decode: Survey or summary; Absolute amount or percentage; Permanent or revisable. Every system in this chapter picks one side of each fork.

Why it sticks: SAP is what the ryot lost.

2.4 Experiment 3: the Permanent Settlement Act, 1793

Memory hook

BOB migrated from North Madras to Benaras and Settled Permanently.

Decode: Bengal, Orissa, Bihar, North Madras, Benaras (the handout's own line; the first letters BOB lead the list).

Why it sticks: BOB is a person walking from the south to Benaras and settling.

Memory hook

I-A-L: three reasons for the Zamindar owner.

Decode: Ideology (English landlordism, security induces investment), Agrarian capitalism (food security, trade, EIC revenue), Loyalty (traditional leaders buy political stability). Revenue Farmers were rich but outsiders.

Why it sticks: "I AL" is the name of someone who gave the land and kept the loyalty.

Memory hook

66 to 60, then forever: the PS arithmetic.

Decode: ryot's Rs 100, LR 2/3 = 66; Permanent Settlement takes 10/11 of 66 = 60 (base year 1789-90) and freezes it as a figure, not a %. Inflation is why it was set high; the Zamindar keeps every rupee above 60.

Why it sticks: sixty-six, sixty, then the clock stops.

Memory hook

I-C-A-T: why permanent and absolute.

Decode: Incentivise the Zamindar, Certainty for the State, less Administration, Trade-off (so demand set very high: double 1765 or 20% above 1757).

Why it sticks: "I CAT" waits at the Zamindar's door for the tax.

Memory hook

Sun sets, land sells.

Decode: Zamindar misses the evening deadline, so the land is auctioned to anyone who agrees to pay the fixed LR (not the land's value). The District Collector collects.

Why it sticks: sunset is a deadline you can see.

Memory hook

Z-Z-P-S: the four pillars.

Decode: Zamindar owns (Pillar 1), settlement with Zamindar (2), Permanent assessment, 10/11, base year 1789-90, absolute (3), Sunset Clause (4).

Why it sticks: two Zs, a P and an S: Zamindar, Zamindar, Permanent, Sunset.

Memory hook

Remit is a trim, exempt is a zero; Sunset is a sale.

Decode: Prelims 2024: Ryotwari remission = reduced LR (not zero), and not Cornwallis 86-93's; the Permanent Settlement punished default by auction (Sunset Clause). Answer (b).

Why it sticks: a haircut, not a shave.

2.5 Impact of PS 1793

Memory hook

A Clever Visitor Always Takes Rent Legally: the seven impacts in order.

Decode: Auctions (Jotedars and family buy; later loyal), Certainty (LR regular, long term), Vision failed (no investment), Absentee landlords, Tenant at will (patta ignored, abwabs), Regulation VII 1799 (Zamindars regain coercion), Litigation (Cornwallis 86-93 Code).

Why it sticks: seven words, seven points, in the handout's order.

Memory hook

Seize, Imprison, Increase: Regulation VII of 1799.

Decode: Wellesley 98-05 undid Cornwallis 86-93's removal of police powers; the teacher writes "7th Law of 1799". Seize property without court order, Imprison, Increase rent at will.

Why it sticks: "SII": the Zamindar's three new verbs; 7th law, 1799.

Memory hook

C-F-V: why courts clogged after 1793.

Decode: Collector stripped of judicial and magisterial power (disputes go to Diwani Adalats), Fees abolished (reimposed 1795, but the flood had come), Vakils with a rigid appeal ladder (cases prolonged).

Why it sticks: Collector, Fees, Vakils, "CFV" like a court-fee voucher.

Memory hook

Patta needs a policeman: Prelims 2001.

Decode: the Zamindar owed the ryot a patta (a rent agreement) but nobody checked him: option (b), no official check. Same chain as "Patta ignored" in the impact list.

Why it sticks: a promise without a checker is just a wish.

Memory hook

Prelims 2011: "Code, not Calm" (answer d).

Decode: litigation rose because of the Cornwallis 86-93 Code of 1793, not because Zamindars grew stronger, not because the EIC was overlord (that was 1765), not because courts were efficient.

Why it sticks: Code = the cause; the other three are decoys.

Memory hook

Built by dividing, sustained by buying.

Decode: BUILT: Triple Alliance and Amritsar 1809, SA (takes external affairs), Bassein 1802 (Baji Rao II 96-18, factionalism used). SUSTAINED: PS 1793, loyal Zamindars.

Why it sticks: two verbs, two halves of the question.

2.6 The Rent Act of 1859 and the Bengal Tenancy Act of 1885

Memory hook

Twelve years: same plot (1859), any plots (1885).

Decode: 1859 Rent Act: more than 12 years on the same plot = occupancy rights (loophole: evict at year 11). 1885 Tenancy Act (Ripon 80-84's Rent Commission 1880, enacted under Dufferin 84-88): 12 years in total across plots in the village = rights on the last plot, loophole shut, sincerely implemented.

Why it sticks: the dozen stays; the "where" widens from plot to village.

Memory hook

59 Rent, 59-63 Indigo, 73 Pabna, 80 Commission, 85 Act.

Decode: Rent Act 1859 (poorly implemented) → Indigo Revolt 1859-63 → Pabna Agrarian Leagues 1873 → Rent Commission 1880 (Ripon 80-84) → Bengal Tenancy Act 1885 (Dufferin 84-88's tenure).

Why it sticks: the numbers go up in sensible steps: 59, 73, 80, 85.

3 · Ryotwari settlement

Memory hook

SCAM-B: it was a B-grade scam.

Decode: Sind, Coorg, Assam, Madras, Bombay (the handout's own line). The teacher's annexation years: Sind 1843, Coorg 1834, Assam 1824-26, Bombay 1817-19.

Why it sticks: a scam that needs a B-grade ticket: SCAM-B.

3.1 Ryotwari in Madras (1801 onward, firmly from 1820)

Memory hook

Read 92, Munro 01 to 07, Munro 20: Ryotwari's three dates.

Decode: Alexander Read 1792 (Baramahal; Munro under him), Munro 1801-07 (Court of Directors convinced; diluted when he left in 1807), Munro 1820 (Governor of Madras 1820-27). Cornwallis 86-93 = PS.

Why it sticks: Read starts it, Munro twice, Cornwallis 86-93 not at all.

Memory hook

Sam Never Forgets Every Poligar: Munro's five reasons.

Decode: Scottish yeoman ideology (ticked), No big Zamindars in Madras, Finances stressed (3 Carnatic + 4 Mysore wars; PS fixes in amount), Eliminate intermediaries (Poligars), Political stability (non-State actors removed).

Why it sticks: S-N-F-E-P in handout order; Sam = Scotsman.

Memory hook

Fifteen Zamindars, half of Bengal, by 1727.

Decode: MQK 17-27 built super-rich Intermediary Zamindars; by 1727, 15 collected 50% of Bengal's LR; that wealth base made PS viable in Bengal and its absence made Madras different.

Why it sticks: 15 and 50, 1727 echoes the end of MQK 17-27's term.

3.2 Under Ryotwari

Memory hook

Gross is greedy (33-50), Net is neat (Ricardo).

Decode: Phase 1 (Read and Munro, 1792-1820) = 33% to 50% of gross produce, ignoring the cost of cultivation: ruin. Phase 2 = net produce (gross minus costs), the Ricardian theory of Economic Rent.

Why it sticks: gross grabs, net leaves the cultivator his costs.

Memory hook

Crop fails, Money-lender, Default, Land gone: C-M-D-L.

Decode: crop failure → borrow from the moneylender at high interest → default → lose the mortgaged land (or default on LR and lose land at government auction). The teacher's end: landlessness.

Why it sticks: the four-step slide down the same slope.

Memory hook

Pringle Pushed 55, Wingate Won 35, Madras Capped 50.

Decode: Pringle (1830): Economic Rent but faulty, 55% of net produce. Wingate Settlement 1835: fair, annual amount for 30 years, Bombay Deccan by 1847. Madras: Torture Commission 1855, Rules 1855/1864 cap at 50% of net produce for 30 years (SoS Charles Wood, 1864).

Why it sticks: 55 too high, 35 the fix, 50 the ceiling.

Memory hook

A-J-P: the Mirasidar's three hats.

Decode: Admin (LR collection), Judicial, Police: concentrated powers with the DC's lower staff, so Mirasidars oppressed ryots, forced defaults and bought the best lands; the aim to remove big intermediaries failed.

Why it sticks: one man wearing three hats takes the whole village.

Memory hook

Ryotwari: Pays direct, gets a Patta, after the Survey: P-P-S.

Decode: Prelims 2012 answer (c): ryot pays the State directly (settlement), receives a patta, land surveyed and assessed first.

Why it sticks: all three tick, so (c).

3.3 Impact in Bombay

Memory hook

Boom 61, bust 65, riot 75.

Decode: US Civil War 1861-65: Union blockade cuts Confederate cotton, Indian cotton booms; war ends 1865, US cotton floods back, prices collapse; debts (Marwari and Gujarati moneylenders) plus crop failures give the Bombay Deccan Peasant Riots of 1875; Patils lost status under Ryotwari.

Why it sticks: three two-digit beats: 61, 65, 75.

4 · Mahalwari settlement

Memory hook

Mahal, Taj Mahal, Tea in a CUP: Mahalwari in CUP.

Decode: Central Provinces, United Provinces, Punjab (the handout's chain). Teacher's dates: Punjab annexed 1849; United Provinces = Awadh, taken in two bites (1801, 1856).

Why it sticks: Taj Mahal sounds like Mahal; tea in a CUP is the picture.

Memory hook

Names climb: 77, 02, 37, 50.

Decode: 1877 North-Western Provinces and Oudh → 1902 United Provinces of Agra and Oudh → 1937 UP → 1950 Uttar Pradesh. NWP itself dates from 1836.

Why it sticks: four numbers, each shorter than the name before.

4.1 Origin in Awadh

Memory hook

Two Seventh Laws: 1799 empowers, 1822 creates.

Decode: Regulation VII of 1799 (Wellesley 98-05): Zamindars get coercion back. Regulation VII of 1822 (Holt Mackenzie): Mahalwari. The teacher calls both "7th Law".

Why it sticks: same number, two Acts, 23 years apart.

4.2 The hybrid structure

Memory hook

Mahalwari = Ryotwari minus one, PS minus one.

Decode: ryot-as-owner areas: like Ryotwari but the settlement is with the Mahal (via the Lambardar). Taluqdar-as-owner areas: like PS 1793 but assessment is not permanent, and ryots' patta rights have legal backing.

Why it sticks: each parent is missing exactly one part.

4.3 The Revised Mahalwari System (R.M. Bird, 1833)

Memory hook

M-C-A-6: Bird's four rules.

Decode: Mahal-level assessment, Community splits the share, Auction of individual ryot lands if the Mahal falls short, 66% of net assets for 30 years, revisable. Dalhousie 48-56 carried it to Punjab and Central India.

Why it sticks: Bird says "MCA 6": 66 is the number to keep (against Madras's 50).

Memory hook

Mackenzie 22, Bird 33: two-two, three-three.

Decode: Holt Mackenzie, Regulation VII of 1822; R.M. Bird, Revised Mahalwari 1833 (NWP 1836 follows); 66% against Madras's 50%.

Why it sticks: repeated digits, in the teacher's order of the two architects.

4.4 Why Mahalwari failed

Memory hook

Crush, Summary, Landless: the road to 1857 in Awadh.

Decode: Crushed Taluqdars (1801-22), Summary settlement 1856 (no survey, unjust LR), Landlessness of ryots; together they fed the 1857 revolt in the NWP and Awadh.

Why it sticks: three words, one fire.

5 · Commercialisation of agriculture

Memory hook

Cash Calls, Crops Change, Larders Fall: C-C-L-F.

Decode: Cash revenue demand → Cash crops (indigo, cotton, opium, jute, sugarcane) → Less food grain in reserve → Famines of the late 19th century. (The handout's hook: "Cash revenue demand → cash crops → food security collapse → late-19th-century famines".)

Why it sticks: each C or L or F is a link you can say aloud.

Memory hook

I Cook Only Juicy Sweets: the five cash crops.

Decode: Indigo, Cotton, Opium, Jute, Sugarcane (the handout's text list). Rice, wheat and millets lost land to them.

Why it sticks: a cook who picks only juicy, sweet things, not staples.

Memory hook

FG out, RM in: Prelims 2021 (c).

Decode: before the IR, India sold Finished Goods; in the 19th century India supplied Raw Materials, so cash crops (commercialisation) is true; handicraft exports, Indian factories and rapid urbanisation are false.

Why it sticks: the exit of FG and the entry of RM tell the whole story.

6.1 Permanent Settlement against Ryotwari against Mahalwari

Memory hook

Z-Z, R-R, M-M: who owns, who settles.

Decode: PS: Zamindar owns and settles. Ryotwari: Ryot owns and settles. Mahalwari: Mostly ryot owns, Mahal settles (or Taluqdar both). Assessment: PS permanent; Ryotwari 50% net (Madras Rules); Mahalwari 66% net, 30 years.

Why it sticks: pairs repeat, only the last one splits.

Memory hook

66, 50, 10/11: the three assessments.

Decode: Mahalwari 66% of rental value (30 years); Ryotwari 50% of net rental value in Madras (30 years); PS 10/11 of the Zamindar's pre-existing revenue, forever.

Why it sticks: sixty-six is the biggest slice, 50 the middle, 10/11 the odd one, frozen.

Memory hook

Three men, three maps: Cornwallis 86-93 East, Munro South-West, Mackenzie and Bird North.

Decode: Cornwallis 86-93 (English landlordism) = Bengal, Orissa, Bihar, Benaras, North Madras. Read and Munro (Scottish yeoman) = Madras, Bombay, Sind, Coorg, Assam. Holt Mackenzie and R.M. Bird (practical hybrid) = Central Provinces, United Provinces, Punjab.

Why it sticks: compass directions follow the three men.

6.2 Common causes of ryot suffering

Memory hook

H-F-L-P: why the ryot lost everywhere.

Decode: High demand, Faulty surveys, Legal vulnerability (no rights, "tenant at will"), Predatory moneylending. Worse off than under the Mughal patta.

Why it sticks: "HFLP" sounds like "help": the ryot got none.

Memory hook

Title with the ryot, village with the Mahal.

Decode: Prelims 2000: land to each peasant with sublet, mortgage, transfer, gift or sale rights = Ryotwari; settlements at village level = Mahalwari (after Bird 1833). The landlord rows (Jagirdari and Zamindari) are where the handout and slide differ; answer letter (b).

Why it sticks: the two sure rows are the ones you can say in a sentence.

7 · Revision PYQs on the class slides (not in the handout)

Memory hook

60 French, 64 Bengal, 95 Marathas, 49 Sikhs.

Decode: Wandiwash 1760 = French against EIC; Buxar 1764 = Mir Qasim 60-63 + Shuja 54-75 + Shah Alam II 60-06; Kharda 1795 = Marathas against the Nizam; Chilianwala 1849 = Sikhs (Dalhousie 48-56's time).

Why it sticks: each pair of digits goes with its enemy.

Memory hook

Kheda: Ryotwari remits, 1918 refused.

Decode: Kheda (Gujarat, Bombay Presidency) = Ryotwari: a bad harvest earns remission (a cut, not a zero). Statement 1 (no relief in a drought) is the grievance; statement 2 (PS in Gujarat) is false. Answer slide: (a).

Why it sticks: Ryotwari meant relief on paper; 1918 is when relief was denied.

Memory hooks: chapter in one breath

Memory hook

The story of "Mughal Patta to Cultivator's Cash".

The Mughal Khudkasht holds a patta; Zamindars take a commission and pass it up the chain (K-P-I-J).

1765: the EIC gets the Diwani, Reza Khan runs it, patta ignored, drought, demand doubled: the Great Bengal Famine 1770 (Greed, Drought, Double); the EIC goes bankrupt (N-F-E), 1.4 million pounds at 4%, Regulating Act 1773.

1772: Hastings 73-85 auctions the farms (Bid, Bleed, Bust). Then Cornwallis 86-93, the believer, says Z-Z-P-S: Zamindar owns, Zamindar settles, 66 to 60 forever, Sun sets land sells. The ryot becomes a tenant at will (A Clever Visitor Always Takes Rent Legally), and Wellesley 98-05 gives Zamindars their coercion back in the 7th law of 1799.

Munro counters with Ryotwari (SCAM-B; Read 92, Munro 01-07, Munro 20): survey, gross to net, Pringle 55, Wingate 35, Madras 50; Mirasidars wear three hats; boom 61, bust 65, riot 75.

Mackenzie 22 and Bird 33 build Mahalwari in CUP (66% for 30 years), and 1856's summary settlement lights 1857.

Cash revenue calls for cash crops, and the larder empties (C-C-L-F). Rent Act 1859, Tenancy Act 1885: a dozen years.

Decode: 1765, 1770, 1772, 1793, 1799, 1801, 1820, 1822, 1833, 1835, 1855, 1859, 1875, 1885.

Chapter 17: Constitutional History of EIC's British India (1773–1858) (54)open chapter

0 · The chapter on one board

Memory hook

Two affairs, two dates: "Trade 1600, CMR 1765".

Decode: commercial affairs (business, private trade) since 31 December 1600; political affairs (Civil, Military, Revenue) since 1765 (ToA 1765). Britain regulates only CMR, because in capitalism the State does not touch business.

Why it sticks: the sixteen-hundred is the trader, the seventeen-sixty-five is the ruler; CMR is the only thing London touches.

Memory hook

@GG = Governor's Ground; @Br State = Britain's Brake.

Decode: @GG looks at the ground in India: the GG, the Governors, the council, laws. @Br State looks at the brake London puts on the Company: the BoC, Parliament, the Crown.

Why it sticks: Ground is where you stand in India, Brake is what London presses.

1.1 The five drivers

Memory hook

MAPCA: "Money, America, Pocket, Conscience, Ambition".

Decode: Money (1767: 400,000 pounds a year); America (revolution 1765-83, fear of an Indian USA); Pocket of the taxpayer (1772 bankrupt, 1.4 million pounds loan); Conscience, the White Man's Burden betrayed (famine 1770, Nabobs); Ambition of Clive 57-67 and Hastings 73-85 for a Crown link.

Why it sticks: five letters, five drivers, in the order of the table.

Memory hook

Two 7s and a 2: 1767, 1772.

Decode: 1767 the State asks for 400,000 pounds a year. 1772 the EIC is bankrupt and borrows 1.4 million pounds from the State. The 1767 demand comes first, the bailout comes five years later.

Why it sticks: the State first asks, then has to pay.

Memory hook

AR 65-83 beside ToA 1765.

Decode: the American Revolution runs 1765 to 1783 (armed war from 1775, USA in 1783); the ToA 1765 (Diwani for the EIC) starts the same year. The teacher's sketch puts them side by side.

Why it sticks: both begin in 65.

2.1 @GG: provisions shaping the Company State in India

Memory hook

4 votes, 4 Cs, GG waits for a tie.

Decode: RA 1773 council = GG + 4C. Only C1 to C4 vote. GG votes only on a tie (casting vote), so he needs at least 2 councillors with him.

Why it sticks: the GG is the referee, not a player.

2.2 @Br State: provisions shaping the EIC-State relationship

Memory hook

"4-5-4": Directors, GG, Councillors.

Decode: Directors 4 years (not immediately re-eligible), GG 5 years (re-appointable), Councillors 4 years. First names in the Act, later names by the CoD.

Why it sticks: the GG is the middle number and the longest term.

Memory hook

Crown chooses the court: 1+3, then 1774, 1800, 1823.

Decode: Supreme Court Calcutta = 1 Chief Justice + 3 puisne judges, all by the Crown; first CJ Elijah Impey 1774; copies at Madras 1800 and Bombay 1823 = 3 Supreme Courts.

Why it sticks: after Calcutta, a gap of 26 years to Madras and then 23 to Bombay.

2.3 Impact of the 1773 Act

Memory hook

"Distance, Surat, Francis": three failures of 1773.

Decode: Distance (poor supervision from London); Surat (Treaty of Surat 1775 by Bombay without the GG, then the 1st Anglo-Maratha War 1775-82, the "emergency circumstances" gap); Francis (Philip Francis blocks Hastings 73-85, Burke's impeachment, Hastings 73-85 acquitted).

Why it sticks: three words, three headaches.

Memory hook

Clive 57-67 73 and Hastings: both tried, both acquitted.

Decode: Robert Clive 57-67 was tried in Parliament in 1773 and acquitted; Warren Hastings 73-85 was impeached (Burke led it) and was acquitted after spending his life savings.

Why it sticks: both walked free but lost their peace.

2.4 Amending Act of 1781 (Bengal Judicature Act)

Memory hook

"Calcutta, Britons, Fort": the 1781 three.

Decode: Calcutta territory; British subjects in Bengal (official acts of EIC officials go to the GG's Council); EIC property in Fort William.

Why it sticks: place, people, property.

Memory hook

Hastings No police, Cornwallis 86-93 police.

Decode: Prelims 2005: Hastings 73-85 did not set up the British-pattern regular police (F); Cornwallis 86-93 did, in the Cornwallis 86-93 Code 1793. SC Calcutta 1773 (T), IPC 1860 (T), answer (b).

Why it sticks: the officer who won the case file also wrote the police rulebook.

3.1 The concept of Double Government (1784-1833)

Memory hook

"CoD for coins, BoC for the crown": Double Government 1784-1833.

Decode: Commerce = CoD (the EIC's own body, chosen by the shareholders); Civil, Military, Revenue = BoC (the State's body). It ends in 1833 when commerce ends. Not to be confused with Bengal's Dual Government (Diwani to the EIC, Nizamat to the Nawab, 1765-72).

Why it sticks: coins are trade, crown is rule; 84 to 33 is the span.

3.2 @Br State: provisions shaping the EIC-State relationship

Memory hook

BoC = SC-4: "Secretary, Chancellor, four Privy".

Decode: 6 members of the BoC: one Secretary of State (President), the Chancellor of the Exchequer, four Privy Councillors. 1 + 1 + 4 = 6.

Why it sticks: S, C, then a quartet of Privy members.

Memory hook

1773 = info, 1784 = original letters.

Decode: RA 1773: the CoD sends information on political matters to the State. PIA 1784: the CoD sends all incoming and outgoing original letters to the BoC (the Secret Committee of 3 lets the BoC bypass the full CoD). Prelims 2002: the 1773 link = B-4, the 1784 link = D-1 (BoC).

Why it sticks: a summary becomes the real letter.

Memory hook

"Double splits the work (84-33), Dual splits the boss (84-58)."

Decode: Double Government 1784-1833 divides the work (commerce to CoD, CMR to BoC). Dual Control 1784-1858 gives the GG two bosses in CMR matters. Both start in 1784; 33 ends the first, 58 ends the second.

Why it sticks: "Double work, Dual boss"; the end years match the Acts that end the trading (1833) and the Company (1858).

3.3 @GG: provisions shaping the Company State in India

Memory hook

ORDinary = ORDered by law; EXTRA = Extra choice.

Decode: Ordinary member = the law orders the person to sit (the CIC in 1784). Extra-ordinary member = the law only gives the CoD a choice to appoint him (the CIC in 1793, "if CoD wants").

Why it sticks: Ordered versus Optional.

Memory hook

4, then 2 + soldier, then 3 + soldier extra.

Decode: 1773 = GG + 4 civilians. 1784 = GG + 2 civilians + CIC (a soldier, ordinary). 1793 = GG + 3 civilians + CIC (extra-ordinary). Both later forms have a soldier, but 1784's is compulsory and 1793's is optional.

Why it sticks: the soldier moves from a chair that is reserved to a chair that is offered.

Memory hook

Conquest "repugnant" = after two wars: 75-82 and 80-84.

Decode: PIA 1784 says "Conquest in India is repugnant to the wish, policy and honour of this nation." Reaction to the 1st Anglo-Maratha War (1775-82) and the 2nd Anglo-Mysore War (1780-84). The teacher's results: Ma (smiley) and Tie.

Why it sticks: two wars, two dates, one ban.

3.4 Impact of the 1784 Act and the Amending Act of 1786

Memory hook

"No veto, then veto 86": Cornwallis 86-93 wears two hats.

Decode: RA 1773 and PIA 1784 give the GG no veto. Amending Act 1786 gives a veto in extraordinary circumstances and allows GG + C-in-C in one person (not merged, not compulsory). Cornwallis 86-93 insisted and was the first to do it.

Why it sticks: the code 86-93 starts with the Act's year, 86.

Memory hook

"H-B-C-M-R-R": the six firsts in order.

Decode: Hastings 73-85 (GG Bengal 1773), Bentinck 28-35 (GG India 1833), Canning 56-62 (Viceroy 1858), Mountbatten (partitioned India, 15 Aug 1947), Rajagopalachari (first Indian GG, June 1948 to 26 Jan 1950), Rajendra Prasad (first President).

Why it sticks: H to B to C is the Company ladder, M to R to R is the free India ladder.

Memory hook

Prelims 2004: "Hastings 73, Pitt 84; Lytton 76-80 press, Curzon 99-05 partition".

Decode: Pitt's India Act is 1784, Hastings 73-85 sits under the 1773 Act (loose pair). The Vernacular Press Act 1878 is Lytton 76-80's; Curzon 99-05 = Partition of Bengal 1905. The true error UPSC wants: (c).

Why it sticks: the press law goes with the poet-viceroy Lytton 76-80, not the partitioner.

Memory hook

Prelims 2002: "B-4 info, D-1 BoC, A-2 trade, C-3 Crown".

Decode: B Regulating 1773 = 4 (Directors present documents); D Pitt's 1784 = 1 (Board of Control); A Charter 1813 = 2 (India trade ended); C Act of 1858 = 3 (Crown takes over). Option (a).

Why it sticks: the list reads 2-4-3-1 in the order of the option letters A-B-C-D.

4.1 @Br State

Memory hook

"Sovereignty 84-93-13": possessions, behalf, undoubted.

Decode: 1784 "Br territorial possessions" (ambiguous), 1793 "sovereign powers only on behalf of the Crown" (EIC = agent), 1813 "undoubted sovereignty of the Crown". Prelims 2019 ink: "(1784, 1793, 1813)".

Why it sticks: the claim grows from "possessions" to "behalf" to "undoubted".

Memory hook

Head to Crown, body to Company (1793).

Decode: Principal Servants (GG, Governors, C-in-C, Councillors) are appointed by the EIC after Crown approval: the State owns the head, the EIC keeps the body (appointment letter, salary, patronage for lesser posts). So no declaration of independence.

Why it sticks: you cannot rebel with a head that belongs to someone else.

5.1 Context

Memory hook

FG out, RM out, FG in: why 1813 killed the monopoly.

Decode: Before the Industrial Revolution matured: India's finished goods are bought in bulk by the EIC (monopoly makes sense). After: Britain wants India's raw material and sells its own machine-made goods into India (monopoly of a trader does not).

Why it sticks: goods go from India first, then goods go into India.

5.2 @Br State

Memory hook

1813: "Tea, Opium, China stay; Missions, Money (1 lakh) enter".

Decode: Trade monopoly with India ended, but Tea, Opium and China stay with the EIC. Missionaries may enter (licence from CoD or BoC); Rs 1 lakh a year on education; Crown sovereignty re-stated; 20-year renewal.

Why it sticks: three things stay, two things arrive.

Memory hook

Prelims 2019: "Two truths, one future".

Decode: Statements 1 and 2 are true (trade monopoly ended except tea/China; Crown sovereignty, 1784-93-13 chain). Statement 3 (revenue under Parliament) is not an 1813 provision. Answer (a).

Why it sticks: the third claim belongs to a later Act, not this one.

6 · Charter Act of 1833, also the St. Helena Act

Memory hook

Napoleon's island changed hands in 1833.

Decode: St. Helena (South Atlantic, remote, a refuelling and repair port) was taken over from the EIC by the Br State. Napoleon was exiled there after 1815 and died there in 1821. The Act is also called the St. Helena Act.

Why it sticks: the same isolated rock that held Napoleon is dropped from the Company's assets.

6.1 @Br State

Memory hook

"3-3-3": commerce gone, control wide, minister named (1833).

Decode: three @Br State points of 1833: commercial function fully ended (Tea, Opium, China gone; Double Government ends), BoC power extended to all administrative affairs (officers, transfers, postings), President of BoC renamed Minister for Indian Affairs.

Why it sticks: three Acts of trading, supervising and naming.

6.2 @GG

Memory hook

Macaulay's trio: IPC 60, CPC 59, CrPC 61.

Decode: Law Commission 1834 under Macaulay, work done 1837, codes after 1857: CPC 1859, IPC 1860, CrPC 1861 (the teacher adds the Indian Evidence Act). Replaced in 2024 by BNS, BNSS, Bharatiya Sakshya Adhiniyam.

Why it sticks: 59, 60, 61 in a row, C-I-C in order.

Memory hook

UCS 33, CCS 53: lower door first, upper door twenty years later.

Decode: Uncovenanted Civil Services (Group B, Munsifs) opened to Indians in 1833; Covenanted Civil Services (Group A) opened to Indians in 1853.

Why it sticks: the same two digits, 33 then 53, with a gap of 20.

Memory hook

1833: one country, one law, one boss, one Law Member.

Decode: GG(Bengal) becomes GG(India), executive + legislative head of all British India, the Governors of Bombay and Madras lose legislative power, and one Law Member (Macaulay) is added to the council for legislative work. Centralisation peaks in 1833; decentralisation begins 1861 and peaks 1935.

Why it sticks: four "ones" for the top of the centralisation arc.

Memory hook

Prelims 2003: "Macaulay was an Englishman".

Decode: (d) an Indian law member is the odd one out. The first Law Member was Macaulay (English). Trading ended, GG of India in Council, all law-making to the GG: all 1833.

Why it sticks: the exception is the only thing that did not happen.

Memory hook

Prelims 2007: "Clive 57-67 is Governor, Hastings 73-85 is General, Bentinck 28-35 is India".

Decode: Clive 57-67 = Governor of Bengal only; Hastings 73-85 = first GG (Bengal), RA 1773 ("G → GG"); Bentinck 28-35 = first GG (India), Charter 1833. Answer (b).

Why it sticks: Governor, then General, then India: the order of the ranks.

Memory hook

Prelims 2006: "1833 trimmed Tea, Opium, China; 1813 cut India."

Decode: 1813 ended the India-trade monopoly (Tea, Opium, China kept); 1833 ended those three and the commercial function; 1858 ended the Company. Ink: Tea + Opium to China, "1813", F. Statement 1 (1833 ended India trade) is false; statement 2 (1858 abolished the EIC) is true. Answer (b).

Why it sticks: the three ends are three Acts: 1813, 1833, 1858.

7.1 @Br State

Memory hook

"No term, no patronage, no budget": 1853 strips three things.

Decode: no fixed Charter term ("till Parliament specifies otherwise"), patronage lost to the Civil Services Examination (first held 1855 in London) and the Budget under Parliament. CCS opened to Indians.

Why it sticks: the Company loses its calendar, its jobs and its purse.

7.2 @GG

Memory hook

4 provinces + 2 judges = 6 Legal Members.

Decode: Bengal, Bombay, Madras, NWP (4 provincial reps) + Chief Justice of Calcutta SC + one regular judge of Calcutta SC = 6; [GG + CIC + 4] + 6 = 12. Rent Act 1859 = Bengal's member.

Why it sticks: four corners of British India plus two judges make a half-dozen.

Memory hook

Halliday 1854: the Lieutenant for Bengal.

Decode: 1853 Act lets the GG appoint an LG for Bengal; 1854: Dalhousie 48-56 appoints Frederick James Halliday, the first LG, because since 1833 the GG was PM of British India and CM of Bengal.

Why it sticks: the year after the Act, the man who relieves the GG of his CM job.

7.3 Governor versus Lieutenant-Governor (post 1858)

Memory hook

Governor = Gentleman from London, LG = Local official.

Decode: Governor: presidencies, appointed by the Crown, aristocrat, writes to the SoS, has a council. LG: provinces, appointed by the GG, ICS career man, goes through the GG, sole executive.

Why it sticks: G for Gentleman and for Governor, L for Local and for LG.

7.4 Evolution of the council (1833 to 1853)

Memory hook

"3 and 1, then 4 and 6": executive and legislative.

Decode: 1833: executive GG + CIC + 3, legislative adds 1 Law Member. 1853: executive GG + CIC + 4, legislative adds 6 Legal Members = 12 persons. The Law Member of 1833 becomes the 4th ordinary member in 1853.

Why it sticks: the two pairs read 3+1 (1833) and 4+6 (1853).

8.2 @Br State

Memory hook

"SoS 15, seven from CoD": the India Council.

Decode: India Council = 15 members, headed by the SoS, with 7 members of the EIC's CoD absorbed for continuity. It replaces the BoC (1784). The GG reports to the SoS only (Dual Control ended).

Why it sticks: fifteen chairs, seven of them still warm from the CoD.

Memory hook

GG rules, Viceroy represents: "Vice-Captain".

Decode: As GG = ruler of British India; as Viceroy = the Crown's representative (the teacher: vice captain). Canning 56-62 = first Viceroy 1858 (same man, two titles). Powers unchanged.

Why it sticks: the captain is the Crown, the vice-captain walks on to the field.

Memory hook

"Sov, D, C, FA": what Paramountcy keeps for the Crown.

Decode: Sovereignty, Defence, Communications, Foreign Affairs rest with the Crown; domestic affairs with the Prince (565 Princely States). British India = 63% of the territory, 78% of the population.

Why it sticks: "Sov-D-C-FA" sounds like a short cheer; the prince cheers only on the home ground.

Memory hook

"Awadh 56, EIC 58": the irony.

Decode: the EIC annexed Awadh in 1856 on grounds of maladministration; the GOI Act 1858 removed the EIC on the same grounds. EIC formally dissolved 1874. Zafar 37-57 sent to Rangoon.

Why it sticks: the judge ends up in the dock two years later.

8.3 Queen Victoria's Proclamation (1858)

Memory hook

"No-A, Crown, Faith, Free": the four lines of the Proclamation.

Decode: (a) No Annexation (princes rule the 565 PS); (b) Crown administration (Br State rules Br India); (c) Faith: religious non-interference, fair treatment, public service open to race and creed; (d) Free: amnesty to the 1857 rebels (with limited exceptions).

Why it sticks: four clauses a, b, c, d = No-A, Crown, Faith, Free.

Memory hook

Prelims 2014: "Crown, no annexation; trade ended 13".

Decode: statements 1 (no annexation) and 2 (Crown administration) are true; statement 3 (regulate trade) is false because trade monopoly ended in 1813 (1833 finished the commercial function). Option (a).

Why it sticks: the Proclamation is about land and rule, not trade.

8.4 Royal Titles Act (1876)

Memory hook

76 title, 77 Darbar, Lytton 76-80 hosts.

Decode: 1876 Royal Titles Act (Parliament lets Victoria become Kaesar-i-Hind); 1877 Delhi Darbar organised by Lytton 76-80; she had used the title unofficially since 1858.

Why it sticks: Lytton 76-80's code starts with 76, the year of the Act.

9.2 British State responsibility

Memory hook

TMFFEF: six reasons the State owns the conquest.

Decode: Tools (Charter: war and territory), Military aid (Plassey 1757, Royal Military Oct 1857), Financed (monopoly 1600-1813, Bombay for 10 pounds 1668, 1.4 million loan 1772), Follow the money (1,50,000 pounds; 1698 New Company 2 million vs 7,00,000; 1767 4,00,000 a year; shareholders), Empire-builders controlled (Crown approval, binding orders), Foreign-policy tool (1st Afghan War 1838-42, Great Game).

Why it sticks: six letters in the order a to f.

Memory hook

Bombay for a tenner: 1661 dowry, 1668 rent.

Decode: Charles II 60-85 got Bombay from the Portuguese as a dowry in 1661 and rented it to the EIC for 10 pounds a year in 1668.

Why it sticks: seven years from gift to rent, and the rent is a ten-pound note.

Memory hook

1698: 2 million beats 7 lakh.

Decode: In 1698 Parliament gave the monopoly to a New Company because it offered a 2 million pound loan against the EIC's 7,00,000. In 1767 the EIC had to pay 4,00,000 pounds a year.

Why it sticks: the bigger loan wins the licence.

10 · The Governor General across the Acts (Prelims 1996) and the Sepoy Mutiny question

Memory hook

Prelims 1996: "C-D-M-W": Cornwallis 86-93, Dalhousie 48-56, Minto, Wavell = A-B-C-D.

Decode: A (1773) = Cornwallis 86-93; B (1833) = Dalhousie 48-56; C (1858) = Minto 05-10; D (1935) = Wavell 43-47. Option (a) A-3, B-2, C-4, D-1.

Why it sticks: four names in alphabetical order, matching the four Acts in time order.

Memory hook

Mutiny 57 sits inside Canning 56-62.
